# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum VIII

## Funding proposal package for FP228

### Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:

- A funding proposal summary titled "Cambodian Climate Financing Facility" submitted by Korea Development Bank;
- No-objection letter(s) issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);
- Environmental and Social report(s) disclosure;
- Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and
- General documentation of the funding proposal.

These documents are presented on submission by the accredited entity and the national designated authority(ies)/or focal point(s), respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure Policy of the Fund, the information contained in these documents is being circulated on a limited distribution basis only to Board Members and Alternate Board Members to ensure confidentiality of certain proprietary, legally privileged or commercially sensitive information of the entity.

### Note on Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes.
* Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the proposal and that they refer to the same project or programme as described in relevant documents.
* The maximum number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service time line.
* The recommended font is Arial, size 12.

### Upcoming Changes to the Funding Policy

Project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to the applicable confidentiality requirements. Proposals submitted to the IDP Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

## A.21. Executive summary (Max 750 words, approximately 3–5 pages)

The Royal Government of Cambodia is committed to combating climate change and accelerating the transition to a low-carbon and climate-resilient sustainable economy. In response to the increasing greenhouse gas emissions in Cambodia, the country has developed a National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) and an Action Plan for Adaptation and Mitigation Actions in its updated Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) in 2020. It also recognized that transforming NDC into action requires significant financial resources to implement climate change adaptation and mitigation actions, and financing challenges are a major barrier to achieving the NDC targets.

The public budget alone is not sufficient to fund NDC needs, and there are no incentives left to scale up private sector investment in climate change. The CCFF will finance projects that support the implementation of Cambodia’s NDCs and contribute to the achievement of its national development goals.


---
**Page 10**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

institutional and commercial funds to implement Cambodia’s NDC targets. This is recognized in Cambodia's updated NDC and National Climate Strategy as further detailed throughout this funding proposal.

The Cambodian Climate Financing Facility (CCFF) Programme has been designed in response to the institutional need in Cambodia for a dedicated, long-term, blended climate finance facility that can provide both national climate financing and support the development of Cambodia’s low-emission, climate resilient pathways leveraging public and private sector resources. The CCFF Programme will be implemented through two separate facilities: the US$ 10 million Lending Facility and the US$ 9 million Technical Assistance (TA) Facility to accelerate Cambodia's National Development Strategic Plan (NDSP). The Lending Facility provides long-term, blended climate finance to local financial institutions and private sector actors to address immediate needs for financing in priority sectors. The TA Facility will provide technical assistance to support climate projects in priority sectors that targets high-priority NDC sectors for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

To this end, the expected climate impact from the programme is 11.1 million tCO2e (exclued and 1.3 million net avoided emissions) through the provision of financial resources to address immediate needs for financing in priority sectors physical infrastructure to the effects of climate change. The CCFF Programme contributes to Cambodia’s NDC implementation, by providing technical assistance and blended finance to support the development of low-emission, climate resilient pathways.

In addition, the CCFF Programme has strong country ownership. Both, the Lending Facility and CCFF TA Facility will be established and managed locally by an experienced international team. In particular, the CCFF Programme is supported by a local implementing partner, the Agriculture & Rural Development Bank (ARDB), established by the Royal Government of Cambodia and operating as a public-private partnership with the private sector. ARDB has extensive experience in providing financial resources to de-risk local green projects and a specific mandate to enable and crowd-in the local financial sector by incentivizing lending for climate change mitigation and adaptation, including through the provision of blended finance.

The CCFF Programme will support ARDB in the GCF accreditation process, enabling ARDB’s transition to a full-fledged financial institution with direct access to international climate finance for Cambodia. On top of that, the Korea Development Bank (KDB) will lead the provision of technical assistance to support ARDB down the road in its role as an implementing partner for the CCFF Programme. The KDB has extensive experience in supporting programmes in developing countries and sharing lessons learned from their decades long track record of deploying climate finance.

The CCFF TA Facility will be managed by Meikong Strategic Capital (MSC), an investment and advisory company providing technical assistance to support the development of low-emission, climate resilient pathways for Cambodia. MSC has the local market presence and track record in the management of climate focused technological assistance projects. The CCFF Programme is designed with a mandate to catalyze climate finance in sustainable land use and agriculture. In addition, MSC in its role as implementing partner will provide technical assistance on project specific risks and opportunities in Cambodia, as well as public and private sector stakeholder engagement.

The CCFF Programme will engage the Royal Government of Cambodia (ROC) in launching Cambodia’s first national green financing initiative and deploying climate finance at scale nationwide.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Climate context (approximately 2 pages)

Cambodia is one of the most vulnerable countries to the impacts of climate change, as evidenced by several international climate change indices*. The country is particularly vulnerable to floods, droughts, windstorms, and heatwaves. These extreme weather events are projected to increase in frequency and intensity.

The vulnerability of Cambodia's economy to climate change is exacerbated by: (1) economic dependence on climate-sensitive sectors such as agriculture, forestry, and water resources; and (2) low capacity to adapt to climate change given a fragile socio-economic context* and limited access to information and communication technology. The lack of infrastructure, institutional capacity, and technology gaps also limit Cambodia's ability to manage climate impact.

On average, the global temperature has increased by 0.8°C since pre-industrial times (1750) with each decade due to anthropogenic greenhouse gas (GHG) emission-induced warming (see B.1.1 Mitigation Profile of Cambodia). The projected increase in global mean surface air temperature is expected to be between under RCP2.6 and RCP8.5 climate scenario (AR5, WBG)*. These constant changes in temperature are associated with more frequent and severe extreme weather events leading to degradation of the country's critical infrastructure and loss of life, which has a significant negative impact on Cambodia's economic livelihood of its population (see B.1.2 Climate Vulnerability Profile). In the absence of rapid and far-reaching global action to reduce GHG emissions, climate change will continue to pose profound challenges to Cambodia's socio-economic development, putting 84% of Cambodia's rural GDP per capita at risk and impacting 7 million Cambodians (53%) by 2050.

#### B.1.1 Mitigation Profile of Cambodia

*The most significant contributor to increased GHG emissions in Cambodia is the Forests and Other Land Use (FOLU) sector, which experienced a net increase between 2010 and 2016, which continues to the present day. The FOLU sector contributed to an increase in total GHG emissions of 579,280 tCO2e halyearly, largely due to economic land conversion for agriculture expansion, deforestation, and illegal logging. While ELCS issuance decreased by 36% between 2014-2016, the FOLU sector continues to persist in Cambodia. The Royal Government of

*The 2021 ND-GAIN index ranked Cambodia as 152 out of 181 countries, using a score which calculates a country's vulnerability to climate change and its ability to adapt to it. It is one of the lowest scores for both vulnerability (67) and adaptation capacity (49). In fact, Cambodia ranks 130th in UNICEF’s Children’s Climate Risk Index, which is the top third of countries with a high risk to climate change. The report found that Cambodia has a very low score on its ability to adapt to climate change, ranking it as one of the lowest scores for both vulnerability and adaptation capacity.

*The Global Forest Watch (GFW) data shows that Cambodia’s forest cover has been largely dependent on large official development assistance (ODA) and FCI inflows. The FCI percent of GDP, per capita, and total amount have all increased since 2013. This is due to the fact that the government has not yet developed a national forest policy.

*Cambodia’s capacity to leverage domestic public and private sector funding to meet UNEP’s $5 billion and IUCN’s biodiversity action plan (BAP) targets remains limited, with only 4% of total GDP allocated for environmental protection. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

**B**

Cambodia’s NDC is currently taking measures to improve the management and monitoring of forest resources with the updated NDC targeting a reduction in historical emissions by 2030.

The second largest emitting sector in the country is the agriculture sector, followed by the energy and industry sectors. The energy sector is projected to see an increase in emissions from the energy, waste, and industries sectors. In the energy sector, the main contributor to increased emissions will be the power generation sector (58%), followed by the transport sector (30%) and the industrial sector (6%). Agriculture (49%), industry (Manufacture Product and Process Use) (27%), and land use change and forestry (14%) are also projected to contribute significantly. Reducing energy sector emissions from fossil fuels is a key priority for Cambodia’s updated NDC, as it represents 58% of the country’s total GHG emissions.

In the updated NDC, the RGGI targets GHG emission reductions in the power generation and transport sectors. It is expected to provide the major share (59%) of emission reductions from the energy sector by 2030. The agriculture sector will also contribute significantly. Reducing energy sector emissions from fossil fuels is a key priority for Cambodia’s updated NDC, as it represents 58% of the country’s total GHG emissions.

The total financial need for mitigation action as per Cambodia’s NDC is US$8.5 billion. To support the RGGI to leverage additional private sector investment in the power generation and transport sectors, as well as in key sectors; however, investment will focus on the FOLU agriculture and energy sectors, as these have the highest impact potential per a Cambodian NDC. CCFD investments expect to avoid a total 11.1 million tCO2e by 2030. The 2nd largest emitting sector in Cambodia is the Agriculture sector.

B.3.2.4.1: Mitigation Action

* **Agriculture, forestry and water resources:** Cambodia’s agriculture sector is already exposed to floods and droughts. Based on a 20-year historical data, losses in agricultural production are mainly due to flooding (about 50% of the total) followed by drought (43%). The average yield for rice has decreased from about 1 ton/ha to fall up to 78% of current yield levels. Similarly, for dry seasons, (irrigated) rice yields could decrease by half and maize yields by more than a third. In addition, the country is highly vulnerable to climate change as over 90% of the population derives its livelihood from agriculture, the degree of exposure to climate extremes and associated weather events is high. The NDC proposes several actions in this sector including: (1) investments to promote the development of the agricultural sector through productivity enhancement, diversification, and value addition; (2) investments to increase access to commercial agriculture, increasing private sector engagement and export potential of the sector[1].

[1] Cambodia’s average annual gross crop growth (AGGR) was 7.2% in 2010-2016. The population increased steadily at an AGRR of 1.5% from 2010 to 2018, with the population growing by 4.2% per year, faster than the rate for the region. Final energy consumption grew by 3.7% per year (2010-2016). Electricity consumption increased at an average annual growth of 5.9%, while natural gas consumption increased at a rate of 3%. It is noteworthy that the mention that the mention Khmer locals lack access to grid electricity and are reliant on wood, or other traditional fuels for energy.

[2] Phong Phan, Sam Sremon, Lim Picharae and Ouch Chhoun (June 2014). Adaptation Capacity of Rural People in the Main Agricultural Areas of Cambodia. Ministry of Agriculture Forestry and Fisheries, Royal Government of Cambodia. The majority of the population is still engaged in agriculture. Over 90% of the population lives in rural areas and most of them depend on rainfed agriculture for living.

[3] Cambodia’s agricultural sector is highly dependent on neighboring countries such as Thailand and Viet Nam, and with regard to demand; as rice export makes primary due to private sector under investment. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 6

## Forests in Cambodia currently have a water deficit period of 4 – 6 months. As per Cambodia’s updated NDC, these forests will become exposed to a water deficit period of 8 – more months. For instance, the Tonle Sap River Basin (November - April) in Cambodia will be reduced by 25%, thereby exacerbating the water deficit already being experienced across the country. In addition, Cambodia is already experiencing seasonal water scarcity in the river basins of the Tonle Sap River Basin. More than two million people living in Cambodia were affected by droughts in 2016, for example, with around 2.5 million people lacking water and existing infrastructure unable to provide sufficient water supply.

### Damage to Livelihoods (Water): Climate change impacts such as increased incidence of drought, extreme rainfall, flood, and sea-level rise will have a significant impact on Cambodia’s physical infrastructure, such as renewable energy infrastructure (solar, wind) are all significantly affected by floods, higher temperatures, and other climate-related events. The cost to repair or replace damaged assets is estimated at US$10 million per year, while under the RCP 8.5 pathway, this could increase to US$234 million in 2050. In addition, the value of renewable energy assets by increasing climate change in the Cambodia is high. Assuming resilience is not built in the design, construction and operation of these infrastructure projects, there will be a significant risk that they will fail or become less effective over time, which could increase operation costs, worsen the funding gap*, and impact business-as-usual operations from those assets. See also Section III for sector-specific (water, solar) climate vulnerability profile.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

The CCFF will provide investments in climate resilient and adaptive rural water supply and sanitation projects, as well as climate resilient renewable energy infrastructure and agriculture in line with Cambodia's NDCs and the Paris Agreement. The CCFF will adopt a country-specific approach to project selection, similar to the approach of the CCFD; the location specific climate risk and vulnerability assessments will be performed for renewable energy infrastructure assets to be funded by the CCFF, so to identify project specific climate risks and inform the design of projects to reduce these risks. The CCFF will also consider the long-term sustainability of these assets. See also table "Cambodia: Climate Vulnerabilities and Impact" under section D1 for sector specific country vulnerability profile. For more information on Cambodia's NDCs, see Annex 2.

### B.3 Program Goals

To finance climate change mitigation, RCU must mobilize US$8 billion for mitigation actions and US$2 billion for adaptation actions. Domestic budgetary resources (which have been constrained by Covid-19 related) are insufficient to meet the financing gap. The CCFF will provide a catalytic role in attracting private sector contribution to the climate change response in Cambodia as a national priority (Source: NDC 2020). In addition, the CCFF will also support the mobilization of international funding for climate change adaptation and mitigation actions.

Cambodia's climate change finance needs largely depends on Cambodia's ability to mobilize international funding from donors. International development partners have committed US$15 billion in climate change financing over 2018-2023, with a response (56% in 2019, but only US$15 million). However, fluctuations in external climate change financing are always subject to donor priorities and funding availability. The CCFF will provide a catalytic role in expanding, as identified in Cambodia's NDC, Climate Change Strategy Plan (CCSP), National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and sector specific Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMA).

The CCFF is designed to support the development of Cambodia's low emission and climate resilient pathways. It is specifically designed to leverage much needed public and private sector financing for key mitigation and adaptation sectors.

### Catalysing funding at scale for key climate mitigation and adaptation sectors: sustainable forest land use (FULU) and agriculture, renewable energy (RE) and energy efficiency (EE), transport and water. The CCFF will provide a catalytic role in attracting private sector contribution to the climate change response in Cambodia as a national priority.

### Additional support

The CCFF will also provide additional support for long-term and concessional financing to local financial institutions and project developers to do risk high climate impact potential projects. The CCFF is designed to support the development of Cambodia's low emission and climate resilient pathways. It is specifically designed to leverage much needed public and private sector financing for key mitigation and adaptation sectors.

The CCFF will also provide additional support for long-term and concessional financing to local banks to change their credit risk appetite and grow their lending to green projects in Cambodia, thereby providing a catalytic role in expanding the climate finance landscape. The CCFF will also provide wholesale loans, as well as co-financing together with the local financial institutions directly at project level; by leveraging much needed public and private sector financing for key mitigation and adaptation sectors.

### Providing technical assistance to facilitate capacity building and knowledge sharing

The CCFF has been consolidated in collaboration with RCG, various donors (including GCF and USAID) and with the support of the Ministry of Environment. The CCFF is designed to leverage much needed public and private sector financing for key mitigation and adaptation sectors.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

## B (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus discussion)

The CCFF is the first national climate finance facility to support the development of Cambodia's low-carbon, climate-resilient economy. It will provide concessional financing for up to $2 billion in mitigation and adaptation projects financed by it. The CCFF Programme consists of the US$100 million Lending Facility and the US$ 9 billion Trust Fund. The CCFF is designed to support Cambodia's efforts towards achieving its NDCs, including post-Covid green economic recovery. In particular, the CCFF provides long-term, blended climate finance to focus on key sectors that are critical for reducing emissions and increasing resilience in Cambodia while also hindering investments into projects with potential to strengthen Cambodia’s climate mitigation and adaptation capacity. The CCFF will support a wide range of activities across all three pillars: mitigation, adaptation and co-benefits such as energy, transport, agriculture, forestry and other land use (AFOLU), and water.

The expected climate impact from the programme is 11 million tCO2eq reduced and 1.3 million direct and indirect jobs created by 2030. The CCFF will also contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions in key infrastructure sectors as well as scaling up and improving enabling environment for low-carbon, climate-resilient development.

The expected climate impact from the programme is 11 million tCO2eq reduced and 1.3 million direct and indirect jobs created by 2030. The CCFF will also contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions in key infrastructure sectors as well as scaling up and improving enabling environment for low-carbon, climate-resilient development.

The CCFF programme intends to adapt to the effects of climate change (1). The CCFF Programme contributes to Cambodia's NDC implementation by providing concessional financing from the Green Climate Fund to fund high priority projects in sectors will be scaled up and the enabling environment improved.

The CCFF programme intends to adapt to the effects of climate change (1). The CCFF Programme contributes to Cambodia's NDC implementation by providing concessional financing from the Green Climate Fund to fund high priority projects in sectors will be scaled up and the enabling environment improved.

The steps required for the realization of CCFF’s envisioned paradigm shift is mapped out in its theory of change (ToC) below, which describes the logical pathways between the assumptions, barriers, activities, outputs and outcomes that are necessary for achieving the achievement of the intended impact or goal.

### Programme Goal/Impact Statement

If CCFF provides programs blended climate finance and support to the enabling environment for Cambodia’s effective use of then GHG emissions across all sectors will be reduced. This includes the energy sector (1.2), the transport sector (1.3), the agriculture and forestry sector (1.4) and the water sector, financial institutions and technical capacity for the implementation of mitigation and adaptation projects.

The steps required for the realization of CCFF’s envisioned paradigm shift is mapped out in its theory of change (ToC) below, which describes the logical pathways between the assumptions, barriers, activities, outputs and outcomes that are necessary for achieving the achievement of the intended impact or goal.

### Programme Goal/Impact Statement

If CCFF provides programs blended climate finance and support to the enabling environment for Cambodia’s effective use of then GHG emissions across all sectors will be reduced. This includes the energy sector (1.2), the transport sector (1.3), the agriculture and forestry sector (1.4) and the water sector, financial institutions and technical capacity for the implementation of mitigation and adaptation projects.

The programme's impact will be achieved through four complementary outcomes:

#### Outcome 1: Increased private sector finance for climate change adaptation and mitigation (MIRA 1.2, 3; ARA 1.2, 4)

Contributing to the realization of Outcome 1 are:
- The achievement of institutional preconditions for scaling up private sector finance in Cambodia’s climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts.
- The development of a national strategy for ARDB as Cambodia's national climate bank (Activity 1.2.3) through the sharing of knowledge from CCFF's operations and its internalization in the ARDB's climate finance strategy.
- The establishment of a dedicated climate finance unit within ARDB to manage the CCFF loan portfolio and developing a long-term strategy for ARDB as Cambodia’s national climate bank (Activity 1.2.3) ii) enhancing the capacity of ARDB to provide concessional loans to private sector entities in Cambodia through loan agreements with selected LFs (Activity 2.1.2), providing wholesale green lending (Activity 2.1.3) and implementing a dedicated climate finance unit within ARDB to manage the CCFF loan portfolio and lending implemented (Activity 2.2.2) through selecting suitable green lending opportunities (Activity 2.2.1), executing loan agreements with selected LFs (Activity 2.2.3), providing direct lending (Activity 2.2.3) and implementing a dedicated climate finance unit within ARDB to manage the CCFF loan portfolio and lending implemented (Activity 2.2.4).

This outcome will be achieved through the activities and outputs contributing to the realization of Outcomes 1 (see above) and 2 (see above). The following projects and outputs will contribute towards this co-benefit:
- **Output 1.2.1**: Strengthening Cambodia’s climate finance framework (MRA 1.2.3, A4.2.2), and supporting capacity building and institutional strengthening for Cambodia (Activity 1.2.1) and Cambodia’s climate change focal point (Activity 1.2.2), and developing a long-term national adaptation plan (NAP) (Activity 1.2.3).
- **Output 1.2.2**: Strengthening Cambodia’s climate finance framework (MRA 1.2.3, A4.2.2) through the sharing of knowledge generated through the COFF programme and supporting capacity building for Cambodia (Activity 1.2.1), and developing a long-term NAP (Activity 1.2.3).
- **Output 1.2.3**: Strengthening Cambodia’s climate finance framework (MRA 1.2.3, A4.2.2) through the sharing of knowledge generated through the COFF programme and supporting climate finance policy frameworks for Cambodia (Activity 1.2.1), and capacity building for stakeholders in Cambodia to prepare national adaptation plans (NAPs) (Activity 1.2.2).

## Outcome: Enhanced climate resilience of the energy, agriculture and water sectors (A4.2.4)
This outcome will be achieved through the activities and outputs contributing to the realization of Outcomes 1 and 2 (see above).

## Outcome: Strengthened institutional and technical capacity for adaptation and mitigation (MRA 1.2.3, A4.2.4)
This outcome will be achieved through the activities and outputs contributing to the realization of Outcomes 1 and 2 (see above). In parallel to contributing to the achievement of the Programme’s outcomes, project outputs and their contributing activities (as described above) will collectively contribute to three co-benefits:
- **Economic development**: Increased private sector investment in climate-resilient projects (Wholesale green lending implemented) and 2.2 Direct green lending implemented) will directly contribute to this co-benefit.
- **Gender and Social co-benefit**: Increased gender mainstreaming into investments. Outputs 2.1 (Wholesale green lending implemented) and 2.2 (Direct green lending implemented) will directly contribute to this co-benefit.
- **Environmental co-benefit**: Reduced environmental impact of investments through the preparation and implementation of NAPs (Activity 3.2.1), and increased private sector investment in climate-resilient projects (Wholesale green lending implemented) and 2.2 Direct green lending implemented) will directly contribute to this co-benefit.

The COFF programme is designed to respond to specific barriers to the achievement of its proposed outcomes and goals, as described below:

### Barrier 1 (B1): Insufficient domestic public resources to meet NDC targets.
Despite Cambodia’s efforts to mainstream integrated climate change in budgeting through the development of a climate change financing framework, yet the public budget alone is not sufficient to fund the NAPs. The total amount allocated for climate change adaptation and mitigation activities in 2017 expenditure was on average 3.8% of total public expenditure from 2009 to 2017 and only 1% of GDP. Cambodia has received both grants and loans from development partners and donor countries to design and implement NAPs, but these resources are insufficient for the implementation of adaptation and mitigation actions. For example, while development partners have been supportive in providing technical assistance and capacity building, they have not provided sufficient funding to fund Cambodia’s climate adaptation and mitigation actions to meet NDC targets.

### Barrier 2 (B2): Lack of capacity, knowledge, and financial resources to design and operationalize climate change policies.
The analyses of Cambodia’s updated NDC and BUR I reveal that, while Cambodia’s future resource mobilization looks toward a reasonable mix of national and international sources of funding, which can contribute towards


---
**Page 21**
---

enabling Cambodia to scale up climate finance, foster innovation and demonstration, as well as generate sustainable development co-benefits along with climate impact. While there are proven climate finance vehicles that can be used in Cambodia, such as blended finance or green bonds, many of these types of such innovative finance models have not been utilized in Cambodia. This is largely due to limited local capacity, knowledge, and resource gaps to implement and access climate finance. Climate finance is yet a new area for Cambodia with no comprehensive overview.

CCFF Component 1 (addressing B1 and B2): Establishment of the CCFF

Barriers 1 and 2 are addressed by the activities targeted under CCFF Programme Component 1: Barrier 1 is addressed through the establishment of the CCFF, which will provide a platform for climate finance capitalization. The CCFF is designed as 20-year programme, to mobilise US$150M loan to high climate impact projects and US$300M equity to support project preparation and co-financing. This will attract private sector investment throughout the Programme duration, given the reinvestment and co-financing features of the loan. The CCFF will also provide a platform for capacity building and knowledge sharing towards enabling conditions to scale up climate finance in Cambodia. The long-term sustainability of this domestic climate finance facility is ensured through (1) creating institutional capacity of ARBIS to design, prepare, and implement projects; (2) establishing a dedicated unit within the Ministry of Environment climate finance department at ARBIS and appointing an Independent Investment Advisor with climate finance expertise; and (3) developing a policy framework for catalysing private sector investment in Cambodia towards a sustained source of climate finance.

In addition, capacity building and knowledge sharing geared towards local financial institutions and private sector actors in Cambodia are embedded in the CCFF Programme design (particularly Component I), thereby building local capacity to scale up access and design climate finance products.

Barrier 2: Risk Averse Local Financial Institutions (LFIs). High perceived risks for climate projects due to limited track record and capacity, no motive to invest in climate projects:

Local financial institutions are risk averse towards investing in climate projects. This is because they require >50% tangible security, and strong pre-existing cash flows** Project finance is not available for climate projects in Cambodia. In addition, the lack of experience with climate finance products in Cambodia coupled with a poor record in climate finance further makes it difficult for LFIs to evaluate investments and develop adequate investment financing approaches. As an example, an increased exposure to climate change risks on infrastructure assets such as dams in Cambodia increases local financial institutions’ perceived vulnerability and impacts integrate climate adaptation into infrastructure project evaluation are significant barriers to the development of a robust climate finance market in Cambodia that can cater for both climate resilient. Furthermore, in the absence of proactive measures to make these infrastructures more resilient to climate change, the increased exposure to climate risks will increase direct annual resource savings for infrastructure financing in Cambodia. This translates into a higher return on investment (ROI) for projects with lower vulnerability and impacts from climate change interest rates ranging between 8% to 16%, p.a., and deposit rates generally between 5% to 8%, p.a. Offshore foreign currency deposits are available at 2-3% above the local deposit rate, but these are coupled with high transaction costs to design the specific climate finance products that have not yet been proven in Cambodia.

In addition, Cambodia’s financial system lacks policy frameworks to incentivise climate finance. For example, Bangladesh has introduced regulatory incentives for the local financial sector to engage in climate finance, such as tax exemptions and subsidies on interest rates. In Cambodia, there is a need to identify potential economic activities to achieve climate change mitigation and adaptation. Indonesia has developed a framework for carbon credits to unlock private sector investment in climate projects and to attract their investor base to channel climate funding through

The document discusses barriers to climate finance in Cambodia, focusing on the limited financial sector and market for climate financing.

### Barrier 4 (B4): Mismatch between available loan terms & climate technology financing needs

#### Component 2: Addressing Barriers B3 and B4: Climate Financing for High Impact Mitigation and Adaptation Projects

Barriers 3 and 4 are addressed by the activities targeted under the CCFF Program Component 2. In particular, Barrier 3 is addressed by providing (1) Wholesome green lending to local financial institutions, structuring loan products that are more aligned with climate finance needs; (2) Reducing transaction costs of LFs's by reducing lower cost funding as well as developing loan products together with the private sector; and (3) Providing technical assistance to financial institutions on high climate impact sectors and (4) Direct green lending to private sector actors, enabling affordable lending rates for projects in Cambodia. The CCFF will provide two types of direct green lending: (1) Green Lending to private sector actors, the CCFF shall contribute to building a pipeline of deals and structuring loan products that are more aligned with climate finance needs; and (2) Direct Green Lending to private, foreign and domestic. Such funding will be granted towards early-stage climate funding or complex project finance (including large-scale infrastructure and energy projects), requiring de-risking through investment-grade credit enhancement.

### Barrier 3: Lack of institutional capacity to identify, data, knowledge gaps to identify, invest and monitor high climate impact projects in Cambodia

At both the level of the LFs as well as private sector actors, there is a lack of institutional capacity to identify low carbon opportunities, assess project risks, and develop appropriate mitigation strategies. This is due to insufficient knowledge of the potential benefits of climate-related investments, and an inability to properly assess and manage project risks, which results in high perceived risk rated for projects.

### Data quality

Data quality is another significant barrier to climate finance in Cambodia. There is a lack of baseline measurements, data collection resources, and methods and information sharing processes that are required to support the identification, assessment, and monitoring of climate vulnerability and climate vulnerability is the biggest challenge, especially in the water sector which is constrained with data gaps. This can result in barriers to identify, invest, and monitor high climate impact projects by LFs and private sector actors.

### Component 3: Addressing non-financial barriers discussed under B1, B4 and B5

The activities targeted under CCFF Program 3 will help address non-financial barriers hindering investments into high climate impact projects as follows:

To derive the above, the COFF's theory of change relies on a number of reasonable assumptions:
* First, it is assumed that regulatory environment and Institutional structures are conducive to private sector investments in Cambodia.
* Second, it is assumed success of some of the policy works under MBI initiatives. Currently ADB, WB, KfW, EBRD, Asian Development Bank and EU have great progress targeting the energy sector gaps to promote Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency uptake through policy interventions, and the CCFF Programme will be a key enabler in this regard. The CCFF Programme is also expected to support development partners who are supporting these various policy initiatives. Furthermore, it should be highlighted that the CCFF Programme is not only targeting the energy sector but also other sectors with issues in the medium term, as evidenced by policy efforts, including for example, a recent plan to design and implement a national strategy on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction which promotes non-hydro renewable energy investments. To this end, theory of change assumption, we reflect upon this and assume that MBI's work will be successful in the medium- and long-term and the CCFF Programme will be able to benefit from these efforts.
* Third, we assume that changes in the country’s political environment does not impact the existing vision of the Royal Government of Cambodia to scale up climate action. CCFF’s investment strategy is aligned with the government’s strategic priorities as reflected in the National Development Strategy (Ministry of Economy and Finance) [MWF] as well as NDC in Cambodia, so the CCFF Programme has policy alignment with the national development agenda. Furthermore, it should be noted that this will not impact existing visa in reflected in Cambodia's NDC and National Climate Strategy.
* Fourth, we assume that the public sector is open to engaging with CCFF team in policy dialogue related to climate finance framework and sector specific policy as it may be required.


Success of some of the policy work under MBI initiatives (e.g., energy sector such as ADB, EBRD, grid connected solar and wind energy projects, etc.) will also contribute to the development of a national rooftop and off-grid access. World Bank: Evs, off-grid access, clean cook stoves; KfW: EE, transmission, regulatory engagement

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

## B. Project programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The CCFF Programme consists of the Lending Facility ($US109 million) and the TA Facility ($US 9 million). It is a fund facility that will provide financial resources to support national governments in implementing climate change mitigation and adaptation projects that contribute to the country's NDC achievement. The CCFF Programme overview is provided in the table below.

### CCFF Programme Snapshot

#### CCFF Structure
- **CCFF Lending Facility ($US 109 million):** Any kind of loan facility with an aim to finance mitigation activities under Component 2 of the CCFF Programme.
- **CCFF TA Facility ($US 9 million):** A dedicated technical assistance facility to fund national and international implementation activities under Components 1, 3, and 7 of the CCFF Programme.

#### CCFF Governance
The CCFF governance has a global governance structure as follows:

| Target size | $US 109 million |
|-------------|-----------------|
| Geography   | Cambodia        |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## Target Activities
- Energy (renewable energy and energy efficiency), water infrastructure, sustainable agriculture and forestry, and low-carbon transportation.

The GCF is requested to participate in KDB's US$50 million senior loan to MEF to support the CCFD Lending Facility and provide US$5 million in the form of grant to support the TA Facility as follows:

- **US$ 5 million non-reimbursable grant to the CCFD TA Facility:** The GCF participation is requested to mobilize additional US$ 50 million investment capital from Agricultural and Rural Development Bank (ARD Bank) and other financial institutions in Cambodia. This will be used for climate change adaptation projects under Component A.

- **US $ 5 million non-reimbursable grant to the CFF TA Facility:** The GCF participation is requested to mobilize additional US$ 50 million investment capital from Agricultural and Rural Development Bank (ARD Bank) and other financial institutions in Cambodia. This will be used for climate change adaptation projects under Component A.

The program output and actions explained earlier in this section contribute to the following outcomes as explained in theory of change (B2) above:

- **Outcome 1:** Strengthened enabling sector finance for climate change adaptation and mitigation (MRA 1, 2, 3, 4; ARA 1, 2, 4): This is achieved through the Interim Outcome 1: Climate financing scaled up across Cambodia for adaptation and mitigation.

- **Outcome 2:** Strengthened enabling sector finance from the AFGLU energy and transport sectors (MRA 1,2,3,4): This outcome will be achieved through the realization of interim Outcome 1: Climate financing scaled up across Cambodia for adaptation and mitigation.

Strengthened enabling environment for improved flows and the use of climate finance for adaptation and mitigation in Cambodia. The following are some of the outcomes that will be avoided or renounced/required:

- **Outcome 3:** Strengthened resilience of policies in agriculture, energy and water sector, strengthening Cambodia's adaptation capacity. This outcome will be achieved through the realization of Interim Outcome 2: Strengthened enabling environment for improved flows and use of climate finance for adaptation and mitigation.

- **Outcome 4:** 1.3 million Direct and Indirect Beneficiaries with increased adaptation capacity to climate change and/or more ability to reduce GHG emissions. This outcome will be achieved through the realization of Interim Outcome 2: Strengthened enabling environment for improved flows and use of climate finance for adaptation and mitigation.

In parallel, the GCF is expected to contribute to Cambodia's sustainable development goals through one or more environmental co-benefits as described below:

### Environmental Co-benefit:
All projects funded under CCFD Programme shall be screened for E&S risks and opportunities. To this end, all projects are expected to contribute to improved land or air or water quality, reduced pollution, increased access to clean energy, and/or sustainable use of natural resources. Furthermore, all projects to be financed from CFF will strive to avoid adverse impacts of its activities on the environment and human health, including those related to climate change, adverse impacts to people and the environment through institutionalizing Environmental and Social practices in project design and implementation.

### Gender and Social Co-benefit:
Projects contribute to gender responsiveness, with [30%] of CFF Loan Utilization allocated for projects that are expected to have a significant positive impact on women supporting gender mainstreamed activities. In addition, projects are expected to bring one or more social sustainability benefits, such as improvements in health and safety, improved access to energy, access to clean water and sanitation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

## Component 1 Establishment of the CCFF

Executive Entity: MIGA, KOR, and ARUB

To ensure long-term sustainability of the CCFF a portion of the GCF grant proceeds will be utilized to establish the CCFF as per best legal, operational and governance practices of the internationally proven climate finance model. The establishment phase is expected to take 12-18 months.

The establishment process will be led by MIGA, as Executive Entity with responsibility for carrying out actions for institutional preparation and implementation of the CCFF.

### Output 1.1: Institutional Preparation for the Programme

#### Activity 1.1.1: Establish the CCFF
This activity shall establish committed facilities (Lending and TA Facility) in accordance with the GCF rules as recorded in Section B.4.1. This includes setting up the CCFF governance structure, such as appointment of Oversight Committee which will have oversight over the CCFF and its operations.

The establishment phase will be led by MIGA and ARUB, and will be supported by a Project Unit Management Team (PUMT) that will provide strategic oversight of the Programme outcomes, outputs and activities, and reporting regularly to both GCF, including KOR as ARUB is an ADB member.

#### Activity 1.1.2: Establish the CCFF Working Group at ARUB
The CCFF Working Group is established at ARUB, the detailed investment framework for on lending and direct investments will be developed in close collaboration with MIGA. This includes credit review, post-investment monitoring policy, CCFF specific gender-screening criteria tool (loan grant specific), and a lower borrower program from procurement and financial management perspective etc., to be developed.

#### Activity 1.1.3: Provide support for the programmematic implementation of the CCFF
This activity includes providing technical assistance to ARUB in order to develop a Programme Manual to implement Activities under Component 2 as per Operations Manual and Credit Policy. The non-exhaustive list of eligible interventions is demonstrated below:

- Project pipeline development: support identification and assessment of green projects and LFs for consideration by the CCFF.
- Due diligence (DD) support for credit review, including E&S and Gender; support pre-investment due diligence assessment, technology feasibility studies and environmental and social management plans as per GCF ESAs.
- Support to ARUB in developing a project pipeline for CCFF investments. This includes technical assistance required to perform high quality DD (e.g. gender screening as per CCFF gender screening criteria to identify women’s economic empowerment opportunities).
- Post-investment monitoring support including MRY: develop internal systems to collect, monitor and report on the performance of CCFF investments in line with GCF reporting requirements. This is for purpose with key stakeholders such as NCMs, or support tracking of NCM contributions. This activity includes post investment impact reporting on an project specific M&E plan.

### Output 1.2: Institutional Capacity Building of ARUB to Become a National Climate Bank in Cambodia

The CCFF Programme has been designed to ensure the long-term sustainability of ARUB to access climate finance and become a leading national climate development bank in Cambodia. The establishment of a dedicated CCFF Working Group at ARUB will contribute to building ARUB's track record in climate finance,

## Component 2Climate Financing for High Climate Impact Mitigation and Adaptation Projects

This component provides climate financing for high climate impact mitigation and adaptation projects in the CCFF target sectors through the CCFF Lending Facility—the ring-fenced loan account managed by the CFFC Working Group. The CCFF Lending Facility will be used to finance projects that are not eligible under other business or assets/liabilities of ARDB, while mitigating any likely risks to the CFFC investors who intend to invest in the CCFF Lending Facility through a combination of risk mitigation measures and an Insurance Programme. Furthermore, the CCFF Lending Facility shall operate as an independent "facility" at ARDB, with dedicated staff and resources for project identification, preparation, appraisal, monitoring, evaluation, Environmental and Social Management Systems (ESMS), Gender Action Plan (GAP), and Monitoring and Evaluation.

The CCFF Lending Facility will be managed by the CFFC Working Group. The CFFC Working Group is a sub-group of the Cambodia Climate Financing (CCFF) Working Group that shall originate, issue, and manage loans.

### The CCFF Lending Facility shall finance projects that meet the following 4 investment criteria:

- **Climate Impact:** Demonstrate a measurable climate change mitigation and/or adaptation impact, contributing to Cambodia's NDC implementation.
  
- **Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):** Contribute towards one or more environmental, economic, or social benefits.

- **Bankability:** Ensure that the project is bankable in financial viability by bankability by investing and delivering high climate impact projects tailored to priority needs in Cambodia.

- **Modelling Climate Finance:** Demonstrate the potential to unlock private capital into the project.

Each of these investment criteria is unpacked in the Annex 21: Operational Manual.

In addition to the investment criteria above, the climate eligibility criteria have been developed to provide a non-exhaustive list of activities in each CCFF targeted sector for classification as climate change mitigation and adaptation projects (see tables below). It aligns Cambodia's NDC in terms of the mitigation and adaptation project categories with the climate finance tracking methodology developed by MDBs/CFM and is consistent with the ASEAN TEGNAP. The criteria are designed to be flexible enough to cover various interventions within a scope of targeted sectors and impact areas.

### Activities Eligible for Classification as Climate Change Mitigation Projects

| Sector | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Renewable Energy** | Electricity generation |

## Sustainable Agriculture, Forestry and Land-use

### Key sub-sector focus:
- **Agriculture**: Rice value chain; industrial (e.g., teak) value chain

#### Energy efficiency in new commercial or residential buildings and performance updates to buildings in commercial and industrial sectors
- Use of building materials with low embedded GHG emissions (including low-carbon cement, insulation, etc.)
- Construction of building structures for high energy-efficiency standards (e.g., double glazing of the building envelope, windows with high thermal conductivity, heat recovery ventilation systems)
- Energy efficiency improvement in assets in existing buildings (e.g., lighting retrofits, heating and cooling systems; heating, ventilation, and air-conditioning [HVAC] systems, water heating, lighting, and pumping and heat exchanger stations)
- Acquisition of energy-efficient or digital smart solutions (e.g., charging stations for EVs, smart meters)

#### Training on upstream energy efficiency and data management
- Other relevant activities in manufacturing industry as per Common Principles

### Note: Energy efficiency measures that extend lifetime of fossil fuel assets are excluded.

#### Sustainable land use and forestry
- Degraded land rehabilitation, erosion control measures, reduced tillage, cover crops, crop rotation, higher inputs of organic matter to soil, perennial cropping systems, conservation agriculture, agroforestry, and other conservation agriculture measures.
- Reduced emissions from agricultural practices (e.g., example: using more efficient nitrogen fertilizers).
- Energy efficient measures, resulting in at least [20%] energy savings compared with baseline.
- Organic input agriculture and bio-fertilizer.

#### Note: Deforestation is an excluded activity; project proceeds will be used to reward farmers for exchange for their protection of forest land.

## Water
Lower-carbon greenfield supply projects that are integrated with or leverage existing local coping mechanisms for water security and supply system. This is mitigated by the climate change adaptation activities planned in water sectors.

The eligible activities could include, but are not limited to:
- Replacement of tanker use for water service delivery to end users with a piped network
- Installation of neighborhood-level pumping stations or surface water collection, or pumping into distribution (piped) networks that can provide water security and supply system.
- Reduction in household boiling or other inefficient household water heating systems
- Requiring point water systems/retrofits to utilize the most energy-efficient technologies available locally for water treatment, pipes, and/or pumping

## Transport
Electric Vehicles and related charging infrastructure

Entity applying for the CCF Fund should demonstrate a net emission reduction as well as ideally a modal shift to low-emission transportation.

The eligible activities include:
- E-vehicles and infrastructure that are fundamental to the operation of transport sectors are eligible.
- Photovoltaic power generation systems of electric motorcycles used by private individuals and commercial operators, including charging stations, including Solar PV powered charging infrastructure.


## Activities Eligible for Classification as Climate Change Adaptation Projects
Depending on the project specific risks, the climate adaptation plan with appropriate mitigation measures and technologies will be proposed for further investment.

### Renewable Energy
- Cooling proof of greenfield solar and wind infrastructure for location-specific physical climate risks and vulnerabilities, at least (but not limited to) heatwaves, droughts, and extreme precipitation.
- Investments into RE energy storage systems to accommodate the variability of renewable energy sources.
- Callings and components that can deal with high moisture temperatures.
- Wind turbines designed to withstand extreme hot temperatures.
- Solar panels and other materials designed to withstand peaks of very high temperature.
- Temperature sensors and other devices for better photovoltaic mounting structures, reducing panel temperature and increasing power output

As a GHG tool, CCF will capitalize on ADRA's climate risk management approach and its sector specific guidelines for climate proofing of the investment. The assessment can range from a simple desk analysis to a complex assessment based on custom climate projections to enable a more detailed assessment.

## CCFF Wholesale Green Loan Borrower Eligibility Assessment Framework

### Eligibility Criteria
- The CCFF wholesale green loan eligible borrowers are local financial institutions in Cambodia that meet the following criteria:
    - Compliance to the National Bank of Cambodia regulatory and procedural requirements (including AMLOCT and corporate governance regulations).
    - Have a minimum of 3 years of experience in lending.
    - Have a track record of building a green lending portfolio.
    - Meet the CCFF’s environmental and social management system (ESMS) requirements.

### Selection Process
- The CCFF will issue a request for proposals (RFP) to Cambodian registered financial institutions via the Association of Banks and the Cambodia Sustainable Finance Initiative, requesting submission of relevant documents as per the RFP.
- Local Financial Institutions will be required to submit all documents requested in the RFP and ensure that relevant management staff are available to participate in the due diligence process, as required.

### Eligible local financial institutions will be identified through the following process:
- Only financial institutions registered in Cambodia and holding a license from the NBC
- Initial screening and assessment of the proposed project concept against investment criteria

The local financial institutions will be asked to submit a project information memorandum, summarizing proposed projects/programmes for the CCFF Facility. The feasibility of the concept is aligned with the CCFF objectives and investment criteria.

### Local financial institutions that pass initial screening will be subject to due diligence and will need to provide information including:
- Business Plan aligned to CCFF requirements

*Business Plan template will be developed by MSc within 6 months of the CCFF F&A effectiveness as part of the Component 1 implementation process.*

## Eligibility Criteria

The COFF Direct Green Loan eligible borrowers are private sector entities registering and operating in Cambodia.

### Selection Process

Eligible borrowers shall be selected through the following process:

- Initial screening and assessment of the proposed project concept against investment criteria.
- The eligible borrowers will be asked to submit a project information memorandum summarizing projected programme for the COFF evaluation to determine if the concept is aligned with the COFF objectives and investment criteria.

The entities pass post initial screening will be subject to due diligence and will need to provide information including:

- Environmental and Social Management Plan and/or Gender Policy as relevant.
- Monitoring and reporting arrangements (M&E Plan).
- Supporting documents as may be required, such as financial model and/or impact calculation. The eligible borrower can be supported by the COFF team to perform impact calculations.

The due-diligence process shall determine:

- Alignment of the proposed project to the COFF four investment criteria.
- Compliance with the COFF policy.
- Operational track record and management's ability to deliver their project intended outcomes.

Only projects that meet the eligibility criteria as demonstrated during due diligence will be considered for further evaluation. The final selection is made by the Credit Committee’s further review and lending decision on per COFF Credit Policy. Once the lending decision is made, the selected borrowers are notified in writing to OC.

### Activity 2.2.2: Execute Loan Agreements with Borrowers

Upon selection of the borrower (direct green project borrowers), the loan agreement is discussed and signed between the ARBDS and the borrower. The ARBDS will ensure that all relevant documents are prepared for signing, including the repayment schedule, legal clauses pertinent to ESM compliance requirements and M&E Plan. The standard terms and conditions of the COFF Loan Agreement shall be used as a reference in the contracting process.

### Activity 2.2.3: Onboard Direct Green Lending

This activity includes but is not limited to on-boarding of the borrower and creating loan account, loan disbursement as per pre-agreed disbursement schedule in the Loan Agreement.

### Direct Beneficiaries:

### Activity 3.1.2: Support Climate Finance Policy Frameworks for Cambodia

This activity includes funding the development of a climate finance policy framework in Cambodia to create an enabling environment to catalyse local financial sector engagement into climate finance.

### Output 3.2 Capacity Building for Stakeholders across the Sustainable Ecosystem

#### Activity 3.2.1: Project Preparation Technical Assistance
This activity will support eligible borrowers to access climate finance. In particular, the activity will fund feasibility studies for innovative technologies and/or projects that are not yet covered by CCFF under Component 2.

#### Activity 3.2.2: Institutional Capacity Building of Borrowers
The activity will select supported borrowers (LFIs, private sector actors) post-investment by building their institutional capacity to enhance the effectiveness of climate finance in Cambodia. This includes strengthening the following structures (climate, E&S, gender), operational processes (ESMs, Gender Policy, Gender Action Plan, Gender Impact Assessment, etc.), and technical expertise (e.g., CCFF policy, development of MRV capacity through targeted trainings, tools and templates, etc.). The activity will also fund relevant technical expertise to support borrowers in accessing climate loans. ### B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

This section is organized in three sub-sections: B.4.1 CCFF Programme Flow of Funds and Legal Arrangements, B.4.2 CCFF Lending Facility Flow of Funds and Legal Arrangements, and B.4.3 Trade records of CCFF Accredited Entity and Executing Entities.

#### B.4.1 Flow of Funds and Legal Arrangements

Flow of Funds: The CCFF Programme is the source of the CCFF Lending Facility and the CCFF TA Facility; hence, below diagrams and text describe flow of funds per each facility:

**CCFF Lending Facility Flow of Funds:**

| Step | Description |
| --- | --- |
| 1. | Borrower submits a loan request to the CCFF |
| 2. | CCFF reviews the loan request and approves it if eligible |
| 3. | CCFF disburses funds to the Borrower |
| 4. | Borrower uses the funds for project implementation |
| 5. | Project outcomes are monitored by the CCFF |
| 6. | Project completion is verified by the CCFF |
| 7. | CCFF disburses remaining funds if project meets criteria |

B

GCF and KDB will enter into an FAA in the form of a loan participation agreement (Participation Agreement) for the financing of Component 2 and KDB as AE will sign a CCFF Debt Facility Agreement with the MEF and the ARDB. The terms and conditions of the loan, including the interest rate, shall be determined by the parties in the FAA, to on-lend to the ARDB. The ARDB will open a new (ring-fenced) account at NBC to receive the KDB funding from MEF (for the avoidance of doubt, a separate account will be opened for each lender). Funds flow through the ARDB to the operational account at ARDB and subsequently disbursed to the customer (eligible borrowers).

Figure 2: Detailed Flow of Funds (GCF Funds)

The CCFF Lending Facility has a target capitalization of US$100 million. The GCF and KDB will contractually agree that GCF shall be granted a participation in up to US$50 million in concessionary senior loans provided by KDB in two phases. Phase 1 is a US$15 million commitment based on co-financing from ARDB, and Phase 2 is the remaining US$35 million upon completion of the first phase. The GCF will mobilize the remaining US$35 million from co-financers within the first 24 months of programme implementation. The KDB will provide a loan to the ARDB for disbursement under the CCFF Lending Facility fund. The repayment to the investors (i.e., KDB and other co-financiers) are made based on pre-agreed repayment terms.

The CCFF Lending Facility is expected to disburse the US$ 100 million in funding over five years. The funds from the CCFF Lending Facility are allocated to select Direct Green Lending and Wholesale Green Lending borrowers based on the terms and conditions included in Project Loan Agreements for each approved green loan transaction.

It should be noted that the implementation of the CCF Lending facility is at a 20% loan and the loan provided by KfW which GCF is participating (such loan, the "CCF loan") is revolving in nature; therefore the principal and interest (net of investment income) will be repaid to the CCF Lending Facility account on an annual basis. ## The roles and responsibilities of each entity relevant to the GCF flow of funds for the CCFF Lending Facility are summarized below:

### KOB (in the role of GCF's Accredited Entity)
KOB, in its role as the GCF's Accredited Entity, will oversee the CCFF Programme implementation and will ensure that the CCFF Programme is implemented in accordance with the terms at the GCF Board and ARDB and its borrowers. KOB will be a member of the CCFF Oversight Committee (OC), which will provide policy direction to the CCFF Lending Facility.

KOB is also an observer of the CCFF Green Credit Committee (GCC), which is responsible for approving CCFF green loans, as well as providing technical advice on the development and implementation of the CCFF Programme. KOB will be a member of the GCC, representing its role in the GCF Board and its representation on the OC and GCC; KOB will represent GCF's rights, and monitor the Executive Entity's compliance with the terms at the GCF Board and ARDB and its borrowers. The GCC will be responsible for assessing achievement of the Programme’s targeted outcomes and results as per the CCFF Programme logic framework.

### ARSB (as an Executing Entity for Component 2)
ARSB is a co-Executing Entity for Component 2, involved in origination, issuance and administration of the CCFF Lending Facility. ARSB will be responsible for the implementation of the CCFF Lending Facility. Therefore, for this funded activity, it shall act as an Executing Entity for Component 2.

The ARSB Board will establish the CCFF OC as a sub-committee to provide overall oversight of the CCFF Lending Facility and its operations. The CCFF OC will be responsible for approving CCFF Lending Facility and ARSB Board. The GCC will be responsible for approving CCFF Lending Facility green loans by underwriting.

The CCFF Working Group has dual reporting to the CCFF OC and the ARSB CEO. This double reporting line ensures that the CCFF Working Group is able to perform its required functions in accordance with international best practices.

### MSC (as a co-Executing Entity for Component 2)
MSC will be responsible for performing as the GCF's designated Financial Advisor for Component 2. MSC will dedicate senior managers to the ARSB CCFF Working Group to provide oversight and effectively monitor the implementation of the CCFF Lending Facility. The Financial Advisor will be an independent representative to the GCC, who will be independent from the MSC Staff managing the ARSB CCFF Working Group.

MSC is a co-Executing Entity for Component 2 as the official representative of the Royal Government of Cambodia (RGC) in the CCFF Lending Facility. MSC will ensure that the RGC fulfills its fiduciary duties and financial oversight of ARDB, and to ensure timely passage-through of Funds to ARDB.

#### B4.2 CCFF Programme Governance and Institutional Arrangements


---
**Page 45**
---

The CCFF TA Facility Committee (GFC): comprised of representatives from MSC and KDB (provides as AE, non-voting member). The GFC will approve TA interventions for Components 1 and 3 submitted by the MSC-commissioned consultants. The GFC will be advised by the Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), comprised of representatives of the MSC (providing an advisory role) and KDB (non-voting member). The TAP will provide advice on targeted policy and capacity building interventions, as well as engage with public and private sector entities on knowledge sharing and awareness raising activities. The TAP will have an advisory, non-voting, role.

CCFF Lending Facility Sub-Committee

The CCFF Lending Facility Green Credit Committee (GCC) comprised of appointed representatives from MSC, KDB, ARB and ANDR. The GCC is responsible for the development and implementation of the Programme strategy and targeted outcomes of Component 2 as per the CCFF Programme logical framework. The GCC will be advised by the Technical Advisory Panel (TAP). The GCC is accountable to the ARB and ANDR.

In particular, the GCC operates as per CCFF Credit Policy and ensures that the credit decision-making is based on a comprehensive assessment of the environmental, social and financial risks associated with green loan transactions. The voting members of the GCC will have a complementary skill set, with MSC bringing experience in reviewing credit applications for projects with limited climate impact potential; ARB brings experience in reviewing credit applications in the rural and agroforestry sectors; ANDR brings experience in reviewing credit applications in the urban sector; and KDB brings experience in reviewing credit applications in the energy sector. The GCC will change policies and procedures as required by the CCFF ultimate impact criteria. MSC will be responsible for approving green loan transactions with limited climate impact potential. ARB, ANDR and KDB will provide unanimous vote by MSC and ANDR. MISC effectively has voting rights on projects with limited impact; ARB, as a non-voting member on the GCC will have a right to receive reports on decisions of the committee.

GCC Meetings & Accountability: The GCC meets on a need basis. It is responsible for approval of the credit facility for green loan transactions and for providing advice to the CCFF Oversight Committee concerning approved green loans. It is accountable to the CCFF Oversight Committee, ARB Board and ANDR Board for its decisions and recommendations.

## Figure 4: CCFF Working Group Organizational Chart

The core responsibility of the ARDB CCFF Working Group is the project management of the CCFF Lending Facility, including but not limited to finance and administrative management, credit risk management, resource mobilization, and monitoring and evaluation. The CCFF Working Group will be staffed by ARDB employees (existing full-time employees from ARDB's core units, seconded from other ARDB units, or hired as needed). The CCFF Working Group, led by the Climate Finance Advisor (IA), will effectively manage the operations of the CCFF Working Group as demonstrated in the organizational chart. This includes managing the project portfolio and ensuring alignment with the climate finance and the need to build local capacity through a "hands-on" collaborative approach. The operational structure is designed to ensure that the CCFF Working Group has a Memorandum of Understanding Agreement (MOA), that shall include defined roles and responsibilities as well as investment guidelines.

The expectation is that MSc will support ARDB through hands-on technical capacity building to implement the CCFF Lending Facility, including but not limited to finance and administrative management. The intention is for the ARDB to take over complete management of the CCFF Working Group over time, as it gains experience in managing this type of facility. This transition will be gradual and phased. ARDB's capacity will be reviewed on a periodic basis and MSc will be able to opt out of the Programme only in case of unforeseen circumstances or if ARDB is unable to meet its obligations under the MOA by written consent. The timeline for this transition could potentially align with ARDB becoming a GCF Accredited Entity.

To fulfill its project management obligations, CCFF Working Group will have 5 core teams led by CCFF Working Group Co-Heads, as well as collaborate with the existing ARDB business-as-usual teams and external consultants as further explained below.

### CCFF Working Group team composition and core responsibilities (snapshot)

* Operations: The team is comprised of three positions, managing CCFF operations functions. The function will be led by a senior operations analyst and supported by an operations analyst and operations associate seconded by ARDB.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Roles and Team Structure

- **Risk Management:** The team is comprised of three positions, managing credit risk across the CCF Lending Facility portfolios, including financial institution and direct lending borrowers. The team is led by the Credit Risk Manager and supported by two credit risk specialists. This team will also manage the credit risk of external consultant personnel whose expertise will be built through collaborating with the external consultants deployed.
- **Relationship Management:** The team is comprised of four positions, managing CCF Lending Facility borrower relationships, including lending applications and ongoing loan monitoring. The team is led by the Relationship Manager and supported by three relationship managers.
- **Business Development:** The team is comprised of four positions, managing CCF Lending Facility pipeline development and business origination. The team is led by the Business Development Manager appointed by MSC and supported by three business development associates.
- **Environmental and Social Governance:** The team is comprised of five positions, overseeing the CCF Working Group's environmental and social governance framework. The team includes functions of environmental and social management, monitoring of climate impact generation, and reporting on environmental and social performance. This team will be led by the Environmental and Social Manager appointed by MSC (with climate governance tasks) and supported by three team members (one each for environment, social, and gender).
- **Capacity Building:** The team is comprised of five positions, providing capacity building to ARDB's senior management team. This team includes functions such as training, mentoring, and knowledge sharing.

## Functionality Collaboration between CCF Working Group and ARDB

- **Compliance/AML:** Ensures compliance with KYC and AML requirements.
- **Loan Operations:** Processes and monitors loan teams approved by the CCF Working Group in collaboration with ARDB.
- **Legal:** Manages legal pursuits and legal records, as required.
- **IT Systems:** Provides IT infrastructure and support for CCF Working Group and lending customers, including online channels.
- **Internal Audit:** Ensures compliance of CCF Working Group activities with ARDB policies and procedures.

This functional collaboration between the CCF Working Group and ARDB core teams will result in knowledge transfer and capacity building within ARDB. This will be beneficial not only as an institution supporting ARDB's development but also transitioning into Cambodia's National Climate Bank.

## Executive Summary – CCFF (Component 1)

A proven track record of deploying climate finance while sectorally inclusive regarding renewable energy markets or finances on scale and tackling challenging circumstances of the least developed market in Korea.

### GCF Proceeds Channeling:

- Active participation in the Korean government’s climate change response position in the domestic PMF market: advised and/or arranged a total of 90 renewable energy projects (total installed capacity of 23 GW) as of Feb. 2022.
- On-lending is one of many business areas of the bank. As a policy financing institution, it has been providing on-lending services to other banks and lending experiences under a structured internal system with intermediaries.

### Follow-up management of GCF CCFF Lending Facility under Component 2

- Willing to transfer decades-long accumulated experience and know-how on climate finance and carbon markets to the Cambodian government in business areas with a purpose of the replication and scale up in Cambodia.
- Well-versed in a standard-on-lending scheme with supporting multiple sub-programs and sub-borrowers in collaboration with local FIs in Southeast Asia.

## Ministry of Economy & Finance (MEF)

The Ministry of Economy and Finance (MEF) was established by law in 1996. Its organization and functioning is defined by a sub-decree of the Royal Decree Law No. 02/97 on the Organization and Functioning of the MEF.

- The MEF plays an important role as the main policy-making body that contributes to the government’s economic and financial policy.
- Included in the MEF are the General Directorate of Budget, the General Directorate of Uniform Financial System, preparation and implementation of the national budget, distribution and redistribution of the total national revenues, management of public debt, and formulation and implementation of economic and financial policies. To implement these tasks, the ministry is divided into several directorates.

### CCFF Oversight Committee member

- The MEF has significant experience in implementing donor-funded programs, including on-lending operations, such as ARISDE for development partners (ADB, ADBI, KF, AFD, ADIB).

## Agricultural & Rural Development Bank (ARDB)

**ARDB**, a 100%-government-owned policy bank, was established in 1998 to provide financial services and support the agricultural sector. ARDB is organized as a specialized bank in agriculture and in February 2020 received its commercial banking license at the direction of the National Bank of Cambodia (NBC). ARDB has been operating under a government-owned commercial bank and is fully independent in lending and governing activities, with its own board of directors appointed by the Ministry of Economy and Finance and supervised by the National Bank of Cambodia. ARDB’s operations are subject to the NBC Act No. 01/2009 and are regulated by the NBC and region-specific norms across Cambodia, enabling ARDB to operate in a more inclusive, holistic value proposition.

The key benefit of collaborating with ARDB is as follows: (1) As an existing national policy bank with a development mandate, ARDB is a well-matched partner for the CCFF Lending Facility in terms of its market position and market. The CCFF Lending Facility can leverage ARDB’s commercial banking license to provide financial services to the private sector in a more inclusive, holistic value proposition. (2) As a Government-owned Entity, ARDB is willing to solve complexities between private sector financing and public finance.
