# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## Whole-of-green lending to local financial institutions, structured as concessional, long-term loans, thereby enabling LFTs to engage in climate finance.

The transaction costs of LFTs will be reduced by the use of a single window for project preparation and financing, which is aligned with the CCFF Working Group that was tailored to the risk profile of the potential borrowers.

## Direct green lending to private sector entities, enabling long-term, affordable lending rates for high climate impact projects and mobilizing capital through co-financing. Through this direct green lending by public financial institutions, it will be possible to reduce transaction costs associated with structuring/greening transactions to facilitate the involvement of institutional investors, commercial and private banks, as well as other sources of financing.

The need for concessional funding is justified in the CCFF funding model (Annex 3). It should also be highlighted that the CCFF will not compete with market rates.

### I. Rationale for Concessional Finance: Contribution that is beyond what is available, otherwise absent from the market, and should not crowd out the private sector.
- Creating a new market for green lending by public financial institutions through market development and mobilization of private sector resources, with concessional risk no greater than necessary.
- Financial intermediation: The CCFF will provide concessional funding to public financial institutions and contribute towards financial (commercial) viability. The sustainability can be demonstrated by the relevant project's ability to repay its debt.

### IV. Reinforcing Markets: Addresses the "root cause of market failures hindering uptake of high climate impact projects effectively and efficiently, minimizes the risk of market distortion or crowding out private sector investors, and provides a platform for public financial institutions' financing, co-investing in other investors, co-financiers, and financial institutions.

### V. Promoting Climate Finance: The CCFF will provide concessional funding to public financial institutions in areas of corporate governance, environmental impact, integrity, transparency, and disclosure.

Source: CCFF Investment and Eligibility Criteria (Annex 21).

## B.5 Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Sustainability
The proposed CCFF Programme component structure is designed with sustainability in mind, as follows:

- **Component 1 - Establishment of the CCFF**: The long-term sustainability of this domestic climate finance facility is ensured through building institutional capacity of ARDB to design, issue, and administer green bonds. This will be achieved by establishing a dedicated green bond issuance climate finance unit at ARDB (CCFF Working Group) and appointing an Independent Advisor (IA). MISC will provide technical assistance in the development of the CCFF. Throughout the term of the project, ARDB representatives will work alongside MISC, enabling climate finance to be embedded into ARDB's operations and institutional capacity building at all levels, as well as governance levels, with ARDB represented on the Green Credit Committee and the Climate Change Committee.

- **Component 2 - Climate Financing for High Climate Impact Mitigation and Adaptation Projects**: The CCFF will provide concessional funding to public financial institutions in areas of corporate governance, environmental impact, integrity, transparency, and disclosure. This will be achieved by building institutional capacity at ARDB and the national entity on capacity building and long-term strategy development in order to facilitate ARDB's participation as a GCF Accredited Entity (GCE). The CCFF will provide concessional funding to other international climate funds. By becoming a GCF Accredited Entity, ARDB will be able to increase its access to GCF resources and co-finance projects with the GCF. This will also serve as a signal to other international climate finance funders: ARDB's readiness and attract further international climate finance. The CCFF will provide concessional funding to public financial institutions in areas of corporate governance, environmental impact, integrity, transparency, and disclosure. This green financing, enabled to play a leading role in scaling up Cambodia’s climate finance ambition, is expected to result in increased green lending offered through the CCFF Programme.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 47

## Background and Context

The need for capacity and appetite for green projects in Cambodia. Post CCFF investment, the borrowers (wholesale and direct green loan borrowers) shall be required to report to the GCF on climate impact, including greenhouse gas emissions reduction, energy efficiency, and water savings. The borrower will have borrowee specific capacity building activities supported by technical assistance (Component 3), such as training, workshops, and seminars. The CCFF Programme is designed to support the borrowers in reporting, and channel additional climate funding in Cambodia. Importantly, the CCFF Programme is designed to help borrowers to change their credit risk appetite and grow their lending to green projects in Cambodia, thereby creating a virtuous cycle of increased private sector engagement in green finance. These investments are likely to bring a positive development effect to catalyse private sector engagement in climate action. The CCFF Programme will be implemented through the GCF’s Component 3 (Technical Assistance) project implemented by the CCFF shall demonstrate to the risk aware and opportunistic private sector entities that Cambodia is committed to climate action, and that Cambodian borrowers are able to deliver on time, contributing to changed risk perception and demonstrating opportunities in climate projects.

### Component 3 - Technical assistance to address non-financial barriers hindering uptake of climate action

The CCFF Programme will provide technical assistance to the borrower to scale up climate finance by fostering climate finance policy innovation, building institutional capacity, and providing financial instruments. The CCFF Programme is designed to support borrowers and private sector actors funded by the CCFF. The Component also contributes to addressing critical climate change issues in Cambodia.

### Replicability

The successful rollout of the Cambodian Climate Finance Facility would serve as a best practice for other developing countries in Southeast Asia facing similar challenges and constraints in unifying public, private, and financial sector stakeholders around climate action. The CCFF Programme will be implemented through collaboration of CCFF with LFTs, shall bring an explicit focus on the need for more climate finance to create the critical mass needed to drive climate action. The CCFF Programme is designed to demonstrate that the CCFF funding will contribute to leapfrogging innovative business models and climate technologies in Cambodia, as well as contributing to positive development effect and contributing to replication within the Country’s private sector as well as regionally.

### GCF Exit Strategy

The participation of the GCF in the CCFF shall be repaid with proceeds received from CCFF Programme. The CCFF Programme will be implemented through a 20-year period, with ARDB acting straightforward as the key instrument of the CCFF is loans, tied to specific repayment schedules which will be monitored and reported on by the CCFF team. Over the 20-year life of the CCFF, it assumed capital will be recycled and re-invested.

### ARDB Capability and Capacity Building

Through ARDB capability and capacity build under Component 1, ARDB is expected to assume independent ownership and responsibility for the CCFF without support from the Programme or the IA (i.e., enabling ARDB to become a green finance institution). ARDB will be required to demonstrate its readiness in receiving GCF accreditation. Given the CCFF Working Group is established within the ARDB Green Finance Department, ARDB will continue to receive technical assistance and capacity building from the GDFD Department as ARDB continues to grow its green loan portfolio through additional funding from international climate funds.

Additionally, ARDB will be encouraged to be a national climate bank by KDB under Component 1. In close coordination with KDB, ARDB will assess ARDB’s current state and develop strategies that reflect the findings through the CCFF Working Group. ARDB will also receive technical assistance in building the knowledge that will allow ARDB to expand its business and become a competent green finance institution based on the KDB Green Finance Development Fund (GFD) Capacity Building Program and KDFD Strategy Development Program. Please see Annex 24 for details.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 51

## Examples of innovative technologies and business models to be financed by CCFF

### Energy - Green Factory Accelerator Platform / ESCO Model

The CCFF team is engaged with Sutch Asia, GG&G Garnet Manufacturers Association in Cambodia (GGMAC) to facilitate the development of a green factory accelerator platform. The project aims at scaling up renewable energy deployment in the Commercial & Industrial (C & I), e.g., garment sector in Cambodia. Through a multi-stakeholder approach, the project will support C & I companies in developing and piloting innovative technologies for improving small projects on their premises. The green factory accelerator will thereby enable the much-needed scale-up of renewable energy deployment.

### Sustainable Agriculture

The CCFF team is engaged with a not-for-profit value chain actor to set up scales of production of organic and dehydrogenated rice production in Cambodia. The key innovation of the project is that it is working with 1,500 farmers for the cultivation of organic rice and dehydrogenated rice on their own land. The project will provide technical assistance to these farmers as well as the premium price paid by the firm above market price and upskilling of farmers who commit to the firm's mission. The project will also support the development of a value chain that ensures the sustainability of the communities are conditional on the maintenance of forest cover, which is monitored by satellite images, which are an integral part of the project.

### Water Supply

Across Cambodia there are over 400 private water operators that supply pipe water to households, schools, and hospitals. The CCFF team is engaged with a Cambodian company to support them in improving their service coverage of their network to all households within their areas – meaning that a large portion of households are currently not served by these private water operators. The project will provide technical assistance to the private water companies, can solve some barriers through their innovative B2B approach, where they partner with these operators to improve their service delivery and also support them in setting up new water supply systems for schools and healthcare facilities within the areas are protecting and increasing the climate resilience of these systems through improved design and construction.

### Energy - Climate Resilient Infrastructure

The CCFF team is engaged with a private sector firm that develops and energy developers in Cambodia to provide much-needed concessional funding to scale up leapfrogging of renewable energy technologies as per ARDB needs. It is noteworthy that the ARDB has identified 10 key energy projects, which are expected to be implemented by 2035. The project will be based on findings, CCFF would support the development to invest in a range of renewable energy technologies and scale up their deployment across Cambodia. This is a welcomed investment for ARDB as it will provide much-needed concessional funding to scale up leapfrogging of renewable energy technologies.

### KIB Capacity Building Program

KOB, as part of its management board and the Programme’s AE, will lead the implementation of the capacity building activities (1.2 & 2.1-3.2) for ARDB's KIB capacity building program designed to transfer its technical expertise in climate change adaptation and mitigation to ARDB staff. The project will be focused on demonstrating how KOB has developed and expanded its functions as a development finance institution for over three decades, and how it is now poised to support ARDB’s climate action plans. The project will assess ARDB's current situation and develop strategies that reflect the findings while firmly grounded on ARDB’s strategic plan. This will include developing an implementation plan for ARDB’s climate action plans for its operations across the board, and the green finance department, that will allow ARDB to expand its business and become a competent national climate bank for Cambodia.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 57

## Cambodia: Climate Vulnerabilities and Impact

### Sector: Agriculture, Forestry and Land Use (AFOLU)

#### Climate Hazard
Increasing mean annual temperature by up to approximately 2°C by the end of this century will lead to approximately a 10% reduction in rice yield. This is due to RCP5 being RCP8.5 for Cambodia (CCF, 2018).

#### Risk Impact
Reduced Crop Yield: The projected increase in mean annual temperature and reduced duration of rice crop growth period will result in a decrease in production in Cambodia by approximately 3% between 2020-2049 under high emission scenarios. This is due to increased temperatures leading to earlier harvest dates (MDE, 2015).

Reduced productivity of rice culture and yield: Increased temperature extremes, heatwaves, impacting the growth period.

#### Adaptation Activities
Project location specific vulnerability assessment and analysis, and financing of adaptation activities to climate change and are aligned with national adaptation actions in Cambodia. Indicative examples include:
- Investments in rice crop management practices (e.g., irrigation, fertilizer application) to promote climate resilient agriculture.
- Strengthening cross-sectoral coordination.

#### Adaptation Impact
Hectares of land or forest area conserved and effectively managed to contribute to climate change adaptation practices: 28,979 hectares. Improved water management that will reduce GHG emissions reduction and increase resilience to climate change in rice culture (e.g., through breeding cross-resilient varieties).

Direct Beneficiaries: Improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers.

CCFF Programme beneficiaries at least 40%.

Includes individuals who:
- Increase access to credit for AFOLU sector to finance adaptation.
- Stage/grow capital investments that are climate resilient practices and technologies.
- Access information on decision making regarding climate change, including how to reduce GHG emissions and increase resilience to climate change in the rice value chain.

Project on water management during dry periods:
- Climate information services, such as (MAE) for farmers and analysis tools, etc.

Climate vulnerable households and their families engaged by the project (e.g., supply chain), which will be 10%.

*For rice crops, past yield gaps are real and physiological conditions may worsen as a result of increased levels of heat and higher temperatures. Increased temperature extremes could also lead to more frequent and severe droughts, increasing the risk of crop failure and the spread of these pests. These increased threats may in turn result in increased need for and cost of agricultural inputs.
*To combat this, farmers will require access to improved irrigation systems, better water management practices, and a greater flow of off-farm credit.
*The climate change adaptation activities described above are expected to reduce GHG emissions by 10.5 MtCO2e per year in the period from 2020-2049. This is due to reduced irrigation requirements and increased water use efficiency, leading to significant variation in the availability of water for drinking, household use and irrigation between wet and dry seasons (MDE, 2015).

# D2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Potential for scaling and replication

The design of the CCFF is based on the internationally proven climate finance facility model, which can be readily replicated by local financial institutions in Cambodia as well as in other countries with similar institutional capacity.

### Key components of the Programme

The CCFF Programme design is in its blended finance approach, capitalizing on the GCF grant instrument to address existing constraints and limitations. The Programme will be implemented through three key components:

1. **Component 1:** Building the institutional capacity of a national financial institution (ARDB) to design, issue and administer climate bonds and other innovative financing instruments.
2. **Component 2:** Building the institutional capacity of local financial institutions and the private sector in Cambodia to access sustained sources of climate finance and the private sector.
3. **Component 3:** Implementing specific activities under Component 1 and Component 3 of ARDB, as well as respective measurable indicators under Section 4.5. These include a mix of climate change adaptation and proposed activities contributing to paradigm shift potential.

## Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

The Programme is geared towards technology transfer and capacity building in the areas of knowledge sharing and institutionalization of ARDB, as well as Royal Government of Cambodia (MEF, MOE) who are to be represented on the GCF Board. The CCFF will also contribute to the knowledge sharing and learning process through implementation. Component 2 contributes to the knowledge sharing and learning, as through accessing CCFF funds the private sector can learn about innovative financing instruments and climate change adaptation products/products as per GCF's best practice requirements, thereby contributing to "learning by doing."

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 62

#### Contribution of activities to support the institutional capacity building of the CCFF borrowers, by equipping them with critical capacity and tools to incorporate climate risk and opportunity analyses in decision making, tracking climate-related financial flows, and accessing climate finance.

The Programme aims to facilitate knowledge transfer to interested public and private sector stakeholders through a series of events and products. The objective is to equip local and regional stakeholders with resources to understand how climate finance innovation and mechanisms can be used to support the development of national adaptation plans (NAPs) and enhance resilience and climate resilient pathways. In planning for knowledge sharing events and products, the Executing Entity will consider the needs of the target audience and the specific context in which they operate.

#### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment: At the core of the Programme design is establishing a conducive environment that facilitates access to climate finance by national governments. Particular Component 1 ensures the long-term sustainability of the Programme through building ANDIF’s national capacity to manage climate funds and other financial resources, including those from the CCFF. This access to much needed sufficient and long-term finance, thereby accelerating transfer of high-impact climate technologies and practices in Cambodia.

Component 2 is designed to ensure that the CCFF Fund will not work itself out (as this is already being financed by donors and CCFF is more additional money for ANDIF) and will be used to support the development of NAPs. The CCFF Fund will also contribute towards addressing knowledge, capacity and policy gaps hindering uptake of climate projects through component 2. This will be done in close collaboration with ANDIF and other relevant stakeholders.

It should also be highlighted that the Technical Advisory Panel will advise the Executing Entity (MSSC) on specific TA investments to support the development of NAPs. The CCFF Fund will also contribute towards capacity building for public and private sector dialogue and contribute to local ownership as well as recipient needs in line to country priorities.

#### Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies: The Programme targets the development of climate finance policy frameworks, thereby accelerating climate finance actions in Cambodia and creating policy incentives for the private sector. The CCFF Fund will not work itself out (as this is already being financed by donors and CCFF is more additional money for ANDIF) and will be used to support the development of NAPs.

It should also be highlighted that the Technical Advisory Panel will advise the Executing Entity (MSSC) on specific TA investments to support the development of NAPs. The CCFF Fund will also contribute towards capacity building for public and private sector dialogue and contribute to local ownership as well as recipient needs in line to country priorities.

#### Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans: Cambodia’s “Cambodia National Adaptation Plan Financial Strategy” (NAP-FS) is a key document that outlines the financial strategy for implementing NAP. The Programme will provide additional resources notably from the private sector and through alternative instruments, such as loans. The Programme will also contribute to the development of the NAP-FS by providing technical assistance in developing the NAP-FS. In addition, the CCFF Fund will be used to support the development of NAPs.

#### D.3 Sustainable development (max. 100 words, approximately 1 page)

The CCFF Investment & Eligibility Criteria requires eligible borrowers to demonstrate contribution to sustainable development in relation to the climate impact (Annex 2). As such, the CCFF Programme is expected to contribute to a number of economic, social, gender and environmental co-benefits as summarized below:


---
**Page 70**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 63

## Economic co-benefits, including green jobs: The CCFF shall catalyse US$ 347 million into Cambodia's economy, with investments resulting in increased economic activity and job creation in key priority sectors. This is expected to generate approximately 15,000 direct and indirect jobs over the course of the programme expected to have an enduring impact and is estimated to create 1.0 million green jobs. Beyond this, the CCFF will contribute to a reduction in Cambodia's vulnerability to climate change by reducing its exposure to extreme heat, landslides, cyclones, and wildlife.

## Social co-benefits and gender-sensitive development impact: CCFF funded projects shall contribute to a number of social goals, such as increased access to affordable clean energy (SDG 7), access to clean water and sanitation (SDG 6) and improved health outcomes (SDG 3). The CCFF is expected to create approximately 40% green jobs created because of CCFF investments expected to be held by women. This will contribute to the gender equality SDG target.

## Environmental co-benefits: The CCFF Programme is designed specifically to finance projects that meet climate change mitigation and adaptation criteria as well as other environmental criteria. These criteria require projects to demonstrate a net reduction in emissions as well as compliance to the CCFF ESM. The Programme is expected to bring the following environmental co-benefits:

- GHG emission reduction at scale, contributing to Cambodia's NDC implementation
- Improved air quality (through climate resilient WASH infrastructure)
- Improved water quality (through development of climate resilient WASH infrastructure in rural areas)
- Promoting healthy ecosystems and biodiversity
- Efficient use of resources through promotion of circular economy

At a project level, the CCFF shall promote environmental and social risk management in line to ESMS, aligned to the GCF Environmental Policy, Cambodia's environmental and social regulations, and international best practices.

## D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Cambodia is considered one of the most vulnerable countries to the impacts of climate change due to a combination of its geographical location and socio-economic factors. Cambodia is a landlocked country and while it has experienced impressive economic growth during the past decade, its performance has been largely dependent on large official development assistance (ODA) and FDI inflows. Despite this, 20% of the population lives below the poverty line, with approximately 45 million people (26% of population) in Cambodia remain near poor and vulnerable to falling back into poverty when exposed to climate hazards. The country's economic growth is also threatened by the coronavirus disease (COVID-19), with an average annual GDP growth rate of 7.2% during 2018–2019.

Importantly, the country is highly exposed to the impacts of climate hazards as follows:

| Exposure to hazard | River, Coastal and inland flood | Landslide | Cyclone | Extreme Heat | Wildlife |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Exposure to hazard | High | High | High | High | High |

*Source: ThinkHazard


---
**Page 71**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 64

## Climate Change Can put 61% of Cambodia's rural GDP at risk and impact 7 million Cambodians (43% of population). This is because, more than 70% of the Cambodian population lives in the rural areas and are highly dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods. The country also has a high vulnerability to climate change due to its location. Cambodia experiences almost all types of climate hazards, such as floods, droughts, heavy storms, fire events, and heat waves. These climate hazards have been increasing over time and are expected to increase in the future.

The government of Cambodia (GoC) recognizes that climate change is a critical issue for the country's development. The GoC explicitly identifies natural disasters, particularly flood and drought, as critical factors that have, and continue to, impact the livelihoods of Cambodians.

Climate change has also been linked to rural out migration among the population of Cambodia. Furthermore, the government recognizes that climate change is a major challenge for the country's development and that climate and climate-resilient development requires US $7.8 billion of financing. However, Cambodia faces challenges in accessing this funding due to its low national capacity to prepare and implement NDCs and other related documents.

The GoC has committed to implementing 100% of its NDCs by 2030. The GoC is also committed to achieving the GoC's NDC needs, and there is an identified need to scale up private, institutional, and commercial funds to implement Cambodia's NDCs. Furthermore, as described under sections B2 and B5, the financing of climate projects by the domestic financial sector in Cambodia is limited due to a lack of awareness among Cambodian banks about the importance of climate financing in Cambodia.

The absence of long-term financial, as well as persisting technology, knowledge and capacity gaps, has resulted in low levels of investment in climate change adaptation and mitigation. This has led to increased migration for a climate vulnerable country like Cambodia, may result in economic losses, as well as loss of livelihoods and assets.

## D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Cambodian Climate Financing Facility (CCF) is directly aligned to Cambodia’s NDCs needs, as it invests into high priority NDC sectors such as FOLU, agriculture, energy (RE, EE), transport and water with climate mitigation and adaptation co-benefits. The CCF will provide concessional financing for the development of projects in the public and private sector finance through a blended finance approach. Ultimately, through financing local financial institutions and providing technical assistance to LFs, the CCF shall contribute to the de-risking of climate projects and unlocking additional private sector capital at the project level.

The Programmes fits with the Country’s national climate priorities through its components, which are aligned to Cambodia’s National Climate Change Strategic Plan 2014-2031 (i.e. national climate strategy), updated NDCs and NAPsMAs as follows:

* **Component 1:** (Establishment of the CCF): Cambodia's National Climate Strategy identifies a need for a financial mechanism to support the implementation of its NDCs, which is aligned with the country’s capacity to manage climate finance mechanisms. Furthermore, the analysis of Cambodia’s updated NDC and NAPsMAs identified that there are significant gaps in terms of technical assistance and international sources. The CCF has been designed to respond to national climate priorities and it is the first financial mechanism established by the government for the implementation of its NDCs.

* **Component 2:** (Climate Financing for High Climate Impact Mitigation and Adaptation Projects): Component 2 is designed to finance sector specific needs, both in terms of technology and practice, as described above. The CCF will provide concessional financing for the development of projects in the energy industry. Component 2’s high catalytic, as it has potential to mobilize much needed local and international resources, which can be used to support the implementation of climate change mitigation and adaptation sectors.

* **Component 3:** (Technical Assistance Facility to address non-financial barriers hindering uptake of climate projects in Cambodia): This component is designed to address project data, knowledge and capacity gaps identified in Cambodia’s updated NDC, as well as the Biennial Update Report (BUR). It includes technical assistance for the development of climate change mitigation and adaptation projects, sectoral knowledge and best practices, GHG measurements and accounting, and MRV transparency.

### D.8. Efficiency and Effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Section B.5 describes the additionality of the GCF instruments and programme structure, including requested concessionality to address identified barriers.

The risk of crowding out other private and public investments will be mitigated by two additional factors as described below:

- As described throughout the proposal, the high-priority NDC sectors are largely financially underserved. In terms of direct financing, there is a significant gap between what is needed and what can be provided through a financing gap through financial and non-financial support. In doing so, the Programme strives to unlock additional private sector investment in green projects by providing co-financing for high-priority NDC sectors through partnering with local and international private and public sector investors on a project level.
- Concessional funding will be provided to address affordability constraints of low-income households with the local financial sector to design affordable climate financing products that can reach targeted end-beneficiaries with low socio-economic status. The Programme will also provide technical assistance in developing solutions as well as targeted technical assistance activities so as to enhance their capacity to properly identify, assess and finance green projects, thereby addressing efficiency criteria needs and attract more investment on a project level.

#### Financial Viability of the CCFF Programme and Need for GCF Concessionality

The CCFF will provide concessional funding to address affordability constraints in order to improve its long-term sustainability. The CCFF Lending Facility will provide long-term green loans at concessionary rates of interest. The CCFF Lending Facility will be structured as a stand-alone facility, separate from the CCBF and other GCF facilities. The CCFF will aim to offer wholesale lending to local financial institutions at a cost of funding which incentivizes their participation in green loan products. This will help to address affordability constraints for low-income households with respect to the risk-sharing projects and crowding in other private sector investors. In particular, the CCFF will prioritize co-financing green projects that align with UfA to ensure they are not competing for the same finance.

The CCFF will provide concessional and efficiently deployed funding to meet the needs of green projects. As shown in the figure below, Cambodia currently lacks a highly catalytic lending facility with flexible financing structures, longer tenors, appropriate ticket sizes and suitable risk appetite for green projects.


| NAP 2025 | NAP 2030 | National |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Engaged Borrowers | CCFF & Institutional Funds | Commercial Banks |

#### Cost Efficiency and Effectiveness

Based on the financial model with a conservative approach (see Annex 3), the Programme is estimated to deliver an average GCF cost of US$7.94 per ton of CO2 reduced (see Annex 2). This is in line with the average GCF cost for mitigation activities, which is US$8.05 per ton of CO2 avoided (see Annex 21). This expected GCF cost efficiency compares favourably relative to similar projects at GCF.


---
**Page 75**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 68

From an efficiency point of view, the Programme presents structural competency to leverage private sector capital. Each dollar invested by the GCF is expected to mobilize additional private co-investment at a proportionional level capital at a project level, which is where key leverage will happen. Overall, this 1:1 leverage is standard for Financial Institution focused climate finance programmatic approaches, including ones financed by the GCF.

In addition, each dollar invested by the CCF in projects financed by it, is expected to mobilize additional private co-investment at a proportionional level capital at a project level, which is where key leverage will happen. Overall, this Programme is expected to mobilize US$ 347 million of investments into Cambodia's NDC priority sectors. The CCF is structured as a lending facility with the objective of financing projects and programs that are aligned with the GCF’s strategic objectives and that can generate positive climate impacts. Project proceeds back into high impact climate projects over the Programme's 20-year implementation period (see Annex 3).

The disbursement period of the GCF funds is 5 years from the date of the first Loan Disbursement Receipt, this period is designed to allow a reasonable origination period of both LFIs and private sector projects. In some cases, origination capacity would depend on the mobilization of project preparation financed by activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.2 and (3) revolving returns of the CCF Lending Facility.

E


There is a lack of institutional capacity in Cambodia to identify, monitor and evaluate carbon and climate related investment mechanisms, as well as limited knowledge of innovative technologies and the real cost of these technologies. There is also an absence of baseline data (including baseline information on public finance resources), that hinders climate focused investments in Cambodia and limits policy makers and stakeholders on benefits of climate focused investments.


Core indicator E: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective technology generation and learning through adoption of good practices, methodologies and standards


To facilitate climate informed investment decisions, the project aims at building institutional track record and capacity to capitalize on opportunities for private finance, knowledge products and knowledge sharing. This will equip local and regional stakeholders with tools to understand how consideration of climate impact, ESG and gender considerations can lead to positive investment outcomes.

## Monitoring and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The monitoring and evaluation framework for the program is designed as a multi-layered framework, with monitoring responsibilities shared by the Accredited Entity, CCFF Executing Entities and Project Counterparties (Borrowers) as follows:

### Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

- **Accredited Entity oversight:** The overall oversight to make sure that the Programme operational milestones as identified in GCF log-frame are reached.
  - The monitoring will focus on the Programme performance against impact level indicators under E2. Outcome level indicators under section E3 and E4 of log-frame) the Programme specific indicators (E5) and activities (E6). In addition, the Accredited Entity shall oversee the compliance in line with GCF evaluation criteria and GCF evaluation policy.
  - The CCFF Oversight Committee & TA Facility Committee (see roles/responsibilities of CCF below). The Accredited Entity shall submit annual reports to the GCF Secretariat on data submitted by Executing Entities, as well as undertake independent mid-term and final evaluation of the CCFF Programme as per the schedule below.

| Milestones/Reports | Expected Dates |
| --- | --- |
| Programmatic Performance Report | As per the FAA Effective Date. |
| Semi-Annual Financial Report and Annual Performance Report | As per the FAA Effective Date. |
| GCF Evaluation Report (mid-term) | Within 12 months after submission of halfway point of the Programme implementation period as per FAA timeline. |
| CCFF Programme Final Independent Evaluation Report | Within 6 months after the submission of the Programme completion report |

**Page 104**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 87

## Executive Entity (EE): The day-to-day monitoring of the Programme milestones shall be a responsibility of the Executing Entities through dedicated management units for components managed by ARDB & MSC as EE, for loan component through CCWF Working Group at ARDB; MSC as EE+ for grant component through TA Facility Unit at MSC), who will liaise with the project counterparties (borrowers, TA providers) to institutionalize a monitoring and reporting framework aligned with the CCFF Programme outputs and outcomes. The implementation of the specific M&E Plan is monitored by the CCWF Project Management Unit (PMU) at ARDB and/or MSC on an aggregate portfolio basis. Each PMU/Unit of the Executing Entities will be responsible for reporting independently on an aggregate portfolio basis. Each PMU/Unit of the Executing Entities (CCWF Working Group for loan component, TA Facility Unit for grant component) shall submit the following reports to the Oversight Committee, TA Facility Committee, and KDB respectively as per schedule below.

### Milestones/Reports
- **Semi-annual performance Reports**
  - Expected Dates: 45 days from the end of each reporting period
- **Annual Performance Report (APR)**
  - Expected Dates: 60 days from the end of the year
- **Annual Impact Evaluation Report (IE3 Indicators)**
  - Expected Dates: 60 days from the end of the Year

The roles and responsibilities of the committees are provided below:

| Committee | Description |
|-----------|-------------|
| Oversight Committee, KDB | The committee is responsible for approving project proposals, monitoring progress, conducting interim final evaluations, and submitting Semi-Annual and Annual Performance Reports to donors as per KDB |
| PMU ARDB | Project Level activities are monitored by PMUs of each EE based on components (grant or loan) managed. They will report semi-annually and annually to OC and GFC (Semi-Annually and Annually). Annual Performance reports to OC and GFC include independent verification of 3 indicators |
| PMU MSC | |
| TA Facility Committee, KDB | The committee is a non-voting advisory body for the CCWF portfolio and submits the following reports to the Oversight Committee, TA Facility Committee, and KDB respectively as per schedule below. |

- **Project Counterparty (Borrower 2, etc.)**

### Oversight Committee (OC) is a sub-committee of the ARDB Board and is responsible for the oversight of the CCFF Lending Facility. Hence, for the purposes of the CCFF M&E approach the roles and responsibilities are as follows:

#### Oversight Committee Roles and Responsibilities

- **Overall governance**: Provides overall strategic guidance to the CCFF Working Group (the PMU of the CCFF Lending Facility).
- **M&E Framework and Evaluation Framework**:
  - Oversees the implementation of the CCFF Lending Facility funded activities to monitor progress against the approved CCFF Programme Logframe. This includes Gender, Environmental and Economic co-benefits.
  - Reviews the monitoring deliverables, engages regularly (at least semi-annually) with CCFF Working Group & ARDB (PMU) to ensure that the CCFF Programme is on track against key performance indicators.
- **Advice and guidance**: Provides advice and guidance on monitoring and interim evaluation issues identified by the CCFF Working Group and/or ARDB (PMU). This includes providing input on outstanding issues and provide decision making to ensure delivery of CCFF Programme.
- **Reviews and endorses reports for further submission to donors, including GCF**:
  - Reviews and endorses progress reports submitted by the CCFF Working Group and/or ARDB (PMU) as per pre-agreed schedules and evaluation process detailed under section 3 of the M&E Framework (see Operations Manual).

- **Contribute to decision making on major changes or issues as per legal agreements.**

### CCFF Working Group at ARBS: the CCFF Working Group at ARBS is the PMU for the CCFF Lending Facility. The CCFF Working Group is therefore accountable for ensuring that all project management units (PMUs) are in compliance with the CCFF M&E Framework and CCFF Programme results (impact, outcome, and outputs). Furthermore, they are ultimately responsible for cascading down the CCFF M&E Framework to a project level and ensuring that each PMU has an appropriate M&E plan in place. The CCFF Working Group is also responsible for providing guidance on the different monitoring requirements related to data capture, recording and analysis of ex-post GHG reduction per borrower (wholesale/direct and indirect) as well as other relevant indicators. The CCFF Working Group will provide a summary report on the progress made by each PMU towards meeting annual reports of each project management unit (PMU) in joint Annual Performance Report to the Green Climate Fund in format acceptable to GCF. The report is submitted by MSC (as CCFF Working Group member) to the K2B for further submission to the Green Climate Fund.

It should be highlighted that the CCFF has developed a robust M&E Framework, that provides an overview and mechanism of M&E for the CCFF Programme. The M&E Framework is designed to provide clarity on targeted outputs, outcomes, and impact (including environmental and social, such as gender). The main objective of this M&E framework is:

- Establish key principles, processes and deliverables for effective monitoring and evaluation of the CCFF Programme and activities financed by it.
- Promote ownership and accountability for monitoring and evaluation among the CCFF team and key stakeholders, through defining governance structures and reporting requirements.
- Encourage learning during CCFF Programme implementation.

The scope of the Monitoring & Evaluation Framework is the CCFF Programme and activities financed by it. It can be integrated with ARDB’s existing tools, including the ARDB M&E System, to ensure effective data collection and monitoring. The full M&E Framework is provided in Annex 21 (Operations Manual), while the key information is summarized in Annex 11.

# F: Risk Assessment and Management

## R1. Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### Description of the risk factor

The risk is that the CCFF Programme will underperform because of the ARDB's current limited track record in climate finance, and limited capacity to identify and implement projects. This may result in technical and operational risks.

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

The risk mitigation is embedded in the CCFF Programme design as follows:

- **KDB:** In line with the role of the acceded party, it has performed a capacity assessment of ARDB that revealed that ARDB has a fiduciary standard that enables them to channel CCFF funding. The ARDB is a policy bank functioning as a risk management and monitoring unit for the CCFF Programme. KDB has also established a Memorandum of Understanding with Cambodia Board and Management has a track record in identifying, measuring, monitoring and controls risks inherent in climate finance.
- **The executing entities for the CCFF Lending Facility are MEF, ARDB and MSC:** who have responsibility to implement the funded activity under Component 2. In particular, MSC will be appointed as Independent Investment Advisor (IIA) for the CCFF Programme, while MEF has experience in Cambodia and Mekong Region and has led the design of this CCFF Programme from inception.
- **The CCFF Lending Facility is a ring-fenced facility:** thereby preventing Programme funds from mixing with ARDB’s other lending activities. The CCFF Lending Facility will be managed by an independent unit within ARDB called the CCFF Unit. Importantly, the CCFF Lending Facility will establish a dedicated project management unit (CCFF Working Group) to manage the CCFF Programme. This unit will have its own budget and will be responsible for Project Management System, Procurement Policy, and Monitoring and Evaluation Framework that are aligned to CCFF Policies and Procedures.
- **The organizational structure of the CCFF Working Group (MIP vs the Lending Facility):** is designed to ensure that decision-making authority regarding CCFF funds is vested in the life of the Programme in personnel with sufficient experience. The CCFF Working Group will be led by a representative appointed by KDB, who will hold senior management positions and decision-making authority within the CCFF Lending Facility and be responsible for the overall implementation of the CCFF Programme. This representative will be independent but will have a representative appointed by but independent from MSC that will advise on all CCFF lending decisions. MSC will be the applicable project advisor to the CCFF Working Group, and will provide advice on the feasibility of projects are approved (given loan approvals require unanimous vote by MIP and ARDB). MSC effectively has veto power over any decision made by the CCFF Working Group.
- **Upskilling ARDB staff in climate finance via co-location training:** will be led by KDB, including the training of ARDB staff seconded to CCFF Working Group positions, as well as BAU ARDB staff working with the CCFF Working Group on a regular basis (e.g., AMGFT, compliance, lending operations, management team etc.).

The CCFF Secretariat, supported by KfW, provides secretarial services to the Oversight Committee which supports this function, with KfW bringing deep financial institution, development, and climate finance expertise. This ensures that the CCFF Programme is well-managed, with a strong representation of advice and quickly detect any operational/technical failures for further mitigation.

| Selected Risk Factor 2 | Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium | Description |

Proposed CCFF structure means GCF's exposure to ARBDS is low. However, credit ratings for ARDBS to default on loan repayment commitments to GCF. GCF funds are subject to potential ARBDS liquidity risk as GCF for loans and grants to ARDBS is a significant component of ARDBS borrowings and loan portfolio, respectively, further compounding potential ARBDS credit risk.

### Mitigation Measures

GCF exposure to ARBDS liquidity risk is mitigated significantly by "low" by the following factors, which will grow and diversify GCF's funding sources beyond ARDBS. This includes GCF funding as a proportion of ARDBS committed borrowings and reducing GCF exposure to ARBDS:

- **ARB2019 - 2028 transformation and growth strategy**: in addition to establishing the CCFF, ARB25 is in the process of transforming its business model. This will be achieved through a full year of 10-year transition to become a fully-fledged commercial bank supporting Cambodia's agricultural value chain. ARB25 loan disbursements are expected to continue in 2022 and 2023, with lending growth moderating from 2024 at average between 10% and 15%. ARB25 will also be able to access the capital market through a public offering of its shares. ARB25 will also have government loans, and loans from development financial institutions (including ADB, AFCD and the International Fund for Agricultural Development) which are expected to grow at an average rate of 10% per year. The loan solvency ratio of more than 48% as of 2021, well above the 15% regulatory threshold. As such, GCF funds and CCFF assets represent an increasingly limited portion of ARB25's overall funding and lending portfolio, reducing GCF risk exposure.

### CCFF phased drawdown approach - The CCFF drawdown of GCF funds will be phased over 5 years, starting from June 2019. This phased drawdown approach is designed to ensure that the proportion of GCF drawn down funds would only represent approx. 3% of ARB's total borrowings. This assumes initial CCFF funding of USD$4 million and a maximum drawdown of USD$56 million over five years, with each year in funds in a 1:1 ratio. GCF funds as a proportion of ARDB committed borrowings is expected to peak in 2028 at the full $50 million drawdown. The phased drawdown approach will also ensure that the CCFF's funding and lending growth over the same period ensures this proportion remains relatively low, again reducing GCF risk exposure to ARBDS.

## Diversification of Funding Sources - The CCFF Programme will support ARDB to diversify its funding sources and reduce reliance on MEF funding by diversifying the ARDB's borrowing base. This includes accessing climate finance from international financial institutions, such as the World Bank.

### Capital Adequacy and Reserves - ARDB will maintain adequate capital adequacy ratios (CAR) to demonstrate the bank’s ability to absorb losses and its ability to absorb potential losses. The procedural requirement set by the NBR is a CAR of 15%, as at 2021 ARDB has a CAR of 34%, significantly exceeding this requirement.

### Selected Risk Factor 3 - Category: Credit; Probability: Low; Impact: Medium

#### Description
As a financial institution engaged in lending to green projects and local financial institutions, the CCFF faces credit risk arising from the possible inability of borrowers to meet their loan repayment obligations. This creates potential for adverse effects on the CCFF’s operations, financial condition, reputation, and ultimately ending the CCFF's ability to continue its mandate. The CCFF’s credit risk is mitigated by ARDB’s credit risk management policies which are compounded by the CCFF’s role of de-risking projects to create in private sector clients for offshore products that may negatively impact the repayment of the ARDB’s loan and MEF, and of MEF’s loan.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The CCFF will primarily manage credit risk through its internal credit risk management policies (Annex 21), including through standard lending processes such as due diligence, taking of collateral, and loan monitoring. These policies and procedures have been developed to reflect international best practice (including IFI Performance Standards) and are designed to ensure that the CCFF is able to lend effectively in support of green projects in the Cambodian market.

### Selected Risk Factor 4 - Category: MEF; Probability: Medium; Impact: Low

#### Description
ARDB is a 100% state-owned enterprise. Given the loan agreement with the MEF, ARDB’s ultimate credit risk exposure will be to the MEF. Complied with the management and governance measures outlined in Risk Factors 1 and 2 (including lending decisions by the Green Credit Committee with oversight from the Operations Committee comprising independent members and credit risk professionals), these measures support an assessment of residual credit risk probability of “low” - corresponding to the credit-risk appetite of the CCFF.

#### Further Notes
Furthermore, the use of loans on loan amounts to individual projects and an aggregated sectoral limit ensures the impact of credit losses on the overall CCFF portfolio is limited to "low".

## Description

GCF financing of the CCFF resulting in money laundering, terrorist financing or other prohibited practices.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The ARBIS will apply the following mitigating measures to the Programme as a leading institution in member country of FATF: (for your reference, the FATF does not call for the application of enhanced due diligence measures to be required; these jurisdictions do encourage its members and/or parties to take into account the FATF guidance).

1. Ensuring that all the businesses supported by the Programme follow the guidelines and requirements at all times and in case of any updates/reshapes to them avoid any legal issues that might arise from the perspective of the AMLA.
2. Establishing a Know-Your-Customer (KYC) procedures policy in line with the AMLA to prevent money laundering and terrorist financing by ensuring the verification of their customer’s identities and identification of financial transactions that might raise red flags.
3. Implementing a record management system covering detailed transaction records to assist in monitoring financial activities, making it easier to review the customer’s history, and understand if there’s anything unusual arising from their transactions.

Additionally, loans provided to both direct borrowers (projects) and on an whole basis to local financial institutions will be subject to enhanced due diligence measures as required by ARBIS, in compliance with AMLA policy and Cambodian law. Borrowers that do not meet these standards will not be eligible for funding by the GCF, as such, the risk of GCF funds being used to finance ML/TF is "low"

## Selected Risk Factor 7

### Category: Regulatory Risk
#### Probability: Low
#### Impact: Low

**Description:** CCF Fund Programme targets investments in several sectors critical to Cambodia's NDC implementation. While the country’s regulatory environment is generally conducive to private sector investment, there are sector specific gaps (e.g., a renewable energy policy that could be strengthened) and a lack of clarity on some regulatory matters.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The Programme is based on the following mitigation measures:

- **Policy Dialogue with the public sector:** Together with the existing donors already financing policy initiatives in Cambodia, such as USAID, ADB, JICA, DFAT, and EU, the Fund has grant programmes targeting the energy sector to promote Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency uptake through pilot projects. The CCF Fund Programme will continue to engage with the relevant government agencies and is motivated to address these issues in term medium, as evidenced by the policy efforts including for example a recent draft of a renewable energy policy that could be strengthened.
- **Storytelling:** The CCF Fund Programme will also use storytelling as an effective communication tool. This includes success stories that should make government more open to change. In a similar manner, donors are active in promoting their own successes and lessons learned. Such on-going work will be successful in medium-term and thereby create enabling environment conducive for private sector investments.
- **Blended finance structure of the CCF Fund Programme:** This minimizes regulatory risks through concessional funding to the private sector that incentivises entities to engage in climate projects even in the event of regulatory obstacles. The blended finance structure also provides a platform for project preparation and implementation, which means the work with the Central Bank of Cambodia to support policy work that creates regulatory environment conducive to climate change mitigation and adaptation.
- **Capacity Building:** By providing training, knowledge transfer, and expertise, the CCF Fund Programme can help entities develop the necessary skills and capabilities to navigate regulatory obstacles effectively. Enhanced capacity enables

**Additional Notes:**
Additionally, the blended finance structure of the CCF Fund Programme can play a crucial role in incentivizing entities to engage in climate projects even when regulatory obstacles persist. For example:

- **Financial Incentives:** Blended finance structure can significantly reduce the financial burden associated with climate project implementation and operation. This is particularly important for small-scale renewable energy projects, where regulatory obstacles make them less economically viable in the short term. The funding can bridge the gap between private sector willingness to invest and the need for long-term financing.
- **Risk Mitigation:** Blended finance structure can also help address various risks associated with climate projects. Regulatory obstacles often introduce uncertainties and increase project risks. However, by providing financial support through a blended finance structure, entities can better manage these risks, including those arising from regulatory changes or delays, giving them more confidence to proceed with climate projects despite regulatory uncertainties.

# Mitigation Measure(s)

Mitigating the risk of the CCFF not achieving its intended outcomes due to the high perceived risk of Local Financial Institutions (LFIs) in Cambodia for climate projects will be done through the following ways:

## Capacity Building and Technical Assistance

The CCFF Programme will provide capacity building and technical assistance to LFIs to enhance their understanding of climate projects, including training on project appraisal, risk assessment, financial analysis, project design, project implementation, project finance expenses, and monitoring. The Programme will also collaborate with international development partners to reduce project finance expenses to provide larger support and guidance to LFIs, facilitating their engagement in climate finance.

## Risk Mitigation Instruments

The CCFF Programme will introduce risk-sharing mechanisms, such as co-financing and insurance schemes, to mitigate the financial risks associated with climate projects. The Programme will also work with the Credit Guarantee Corporation of Cambodia (CGCC) to develop a portfolio guarantee scheme to cover potential default arising from climate projects, thereby providing safety net for LFIs.

## Incentive Mechanisms

The CCFF Programme will provide wholesale funding at preferential interest rates reduced collateral requirements and other incentives to encourage LFIs to invest in climate projects and expand their lending portfolios. The CCFF Programme will foster partnerships between LFs and climate project developers to create mutually beneficial collaborations that align with the LFI’s business objectives and risk appetite.

## Knowledge Sharing and Awareness

The CCFF Programme will conduct targeted awareness campaigns and knowledge sharing activities, such as workshops, seminars, and training sessions, to raise awareness of climate change and its impacts. The Programme will share success stories and case studies highlighting the financial viability and positive impact of climate projects on LFI’s business operations. The Programme will also facilitate networking and peer learning platforms for LFIs, allowing them to share experiences, best practices, and lessons learned.

## Policy and Regulatory Support

The CCFF Programme will work closely with relevant regulatory authorities to establish supportive policy frameworks and incentives that encourage LFIs to allocate resources towards climate projects. The Programme will engage stakeholders to advocate for streamlined regulatory processes and procedures specific to climate finance projects. The CCFF Programme will also provide technical assistance to LFI’s in developing their capacity to manage climate risks.

The CCFF Programme will engage in policy dialogue with the government to promote the integration of climate considerations into national development plans and sector strategies.

By implementing these strategies, the CCFF Programme will mitigate the perceived risk of LFIs, build their capacity and confidence in financing climate projects, and encourage them to channel funds towards climate initiatives. This, in turn, will help achieve the intended outcomes of the Programme in promoting climate action and sustainable development in Cambodia.

## Mitigating the risk of the CCFF not achieving its intended outcomes due to investor commitments and insufficient bankable green projects requires proactive measures and strategies that will be done through the following ways:

### Investor engagement and commitments

There continues to be regular communication and engagement with potential co-financers to ensure a clear understanding of the CCFF’s goals and objectives, including clearly communicated project requirements.

### Project pipeline development

There has been significant market outreach and awareness since the CCFF Readiness Study (KHMR-RS-003) that had built expectations in the market for the CCFF. The CCFF has been developed with a strong focus on building a robust project pipeline, which includes engaging developers and sponsors, and financial institutions. The CCFF TA Facility will offer support and capacity building to project developers to enhance their ability to develop green projects and meet CCFF requirements. The CCFF will


---
**Page 116**
---

Adaptability and flexibility: The CCFF Working Group will continuously assess market dynamics and evolving climate priorities to identify emerging technologies with high potential for green projects. The CCFF investment structure will be designed to support a wide range of project types, including those that are innovative or have low likelihood of finding bankable projects. The CCFF will design and offer flexible financing structures that accommodate the specific needs and risk profiles of different types of green projects, allowing customized financing solutions.

Collaboration and knowledge sharing: The CCFF will foster collaboration and knowledge sharing among stakeholders, including financial institutions, project developers, government agencies and industry experts to contribute to a more efficient and effective climate finance ecosystem. The CCFF Programme will organize workshops to facilitate dialogue and learning opportunities among key stakeholders, providing them with the necessary tools and resources for successful project development.

By implementing these strategies, the CCFF can mitigate the risks associated with co-financer commitments and insufficient bankable green projects. Proactive investor engagement, strong pipeline development, capacity building support, adaptability and collaboration can enhance the CCFF's ability to attract co-finance and finance a robust portfolio of green projects.

Description
The CCFF underperforms in the expected uptake of green loans due to private sector indebtedness.

Mitigation Measure(s)
We have already checked demand for climate finance from private businesses. Therefore, the risk of the private sector indebtedness will be limited to this programme. Furthermore, the CCFF will implement responsible lending policies at both the project and portfolio level, including a comprehensive set of credit underwriting standards, loan terms and risk assessment frameworks, improved business viability through TA and capacity building initiatives, and fosters an enabling environment for sustainable finance.

Specifically, the CCFF will have:

- Borrower selection criteria and guidelines, designed to ensure responsible assessment of a project's debt capacity (affordable and suitable to need), ensuring additional debt does not put the borrower into a situation where it is unable to service its existing obligations.
- Policies and guidelines that include requirements for potential borrowers to meet international benchmark ratios for assessment of servicing ability
- Loan agreements with banks that include reporting requirements of these ratios to ensure that analysis of the end borrower has been completed and adheres to responsible lending guidelines.

Part of this will be a commitment not to engage in predatory marketing practices, whereby any institution that engages in any form of predatory lending will be excluded.

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Programme EAS Risk Categorization: The Programme aims to establish a US$109 million Cambodian Climate Finance Facility, so to invest into high climate impact projects in FOLU, Agriculture, Energy (RE, EE and Transport) and Water sectors. The EAS risk categorization is based on the GCF revised Environmental and Social Performance Standards (ESPS), which are generally site-specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed through mitigation measures. The high risk categories (A and B) are excluded from the GCF Fund (see Annex 6). The low and medium risks are included in CFFG & EAS Management System (Annex 6). Provided that the Programme includes categories of activities identified as “high” risk under the ESPS, the EAS will be conducted by an independent third party, as per GCF’s revised environmental and social policy. The portfolio of CFFG shall include low and medium EAS risk projects only.

#### Environmental and Social Management: The Accredited Entity has developed the Environmental & Social Management System for CFFG in accordance with the GCF revised EAS Policy and IFC Performance Standards. The ESMS provides a set of policies, procedures, and resources to identify, manage, and reduce environmental and social risks associated with the Programme’s activities. The implementation of the ESMS and the environmental management process is summarized below; please refer to the Annex 6 (E&S Management System) for details.

##### Screening of activities to be financed by the GCF EAS

| Screening of the activities against ESMS Exclusion List to ensure that the project does not fall under any exclusion criteria |
| --- |
| Risk Categorization |
| Category A & B risks, only category B (medium) and Category C have EAS risk. Category D has no EAS risk. Category A (high) risk activities are excluded from GCF investment* |
| Due-Diligence (DD) |
| Assess E&S risks associated with the project, including Borrower’s capacity to manage the E&S risk; identify potential mitigation measures and resources |
| Decision-making |
| Develop time-bound and measurable borrower specific commitments and mitigation measures for each activity. The ESMS will be used as a tool to assess the environmental and social assessment of the activities, make an investment decision accordingly. Only after the approval of the Investment Committee, E&S risks are finally addressed through mitigation measures as detailed in ESAP |

### G2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

A Programme level gender assessment has been performed to identify gender specific vulnerabilities and risks in Cambodia with regard to the project design and funding. The detailed assessment is included in Annex 8a, and the key vulnerabilities are summarized below:

- The skill and capacity of women’s participation in economic activities remain limited, and the opportunities and resource management open to women remain lower than men, with barriers to access education, training, credit facilities, land tenure, markets, savings, financial services, finances, and technology.
- Limited women's involvement in decision-making across all sectors, which results in underrepresentation of women needs in project design.
- While there is a general recognition that gender equality is a lack of the gender mainstreaming in financial sector and limited financial products incorporating gender inclusiveness.

Based on this assessment, the CCFF programme aims to mainstream gender into its investments, through promoting gender equity and economic empowerment across key programme outputs (Output 1.3 and Section B) as follows:

### 1.1 Enhance Institutional Preparedness of the CCFF: The CCFF is set up as a ring-fenced account with dedicated governance, operational structure (including team/competitiveness and Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV)). Through this institutional preparedness, the CCFF will be able to mainstream gender into its investments. For the CCFF, gender will be mainstreamed across CCFF’s governance, operational process and monitoring, reporting, and verification.

#### 1. Mainstreamed Gender Governance on the level of the CCFF will be achieved through (a) adopting CCFF Gender Policy, (b) establishing a Gender Focal Point at the CCFF Secretariat, (c) mainstreaming gender into CCFF investments (i.e.) empowering women in decision-making roles and setting specific targets and (d) enhancing internal capacity for gender analysis and gender mainstreaming. The CCFF Gender Focal Point will be appointed by an Executing Entity to the CCFF working group, which will be supported by external consultants on a need basis to implement CCFF Gender Policy. To promote gender equality and women economic empowerment at the level of borrower/project, the CIFC will introduce 20% explicit gender-mainstreamed criteria in lending process (see Annex Bb for preliminary criteria) and dedicate 30% of its Lending Facility to borrowers that meet preliminary criteria.

- **Direct Beneficiaries with more climate-resilient water security (748,135), out of which 51% are female.**

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately | page 1)

**KGB will be responsible for the monitoring and review of the Programme’s performance and work closely with the Executive Director and other relevant parties on financial management, risk management, procurement, etc.) to ensure compliance with best practices and international standards. KGB, through periodic reviews of the CCFF Programme’s financial statements, will provide recommendations to the Programme to review financial and operational performance in order to ensure compliance its policies and provide recommendations for improvement as needed.**

The investment processes of the CCFF will be managed according to the general provisions of: (i) The FAA and AMA between the KGB and CCF; and (ii) Financial management and procurement guidelines of the CCFF Programme to be developed by the KGB in accordance with the CCFF’s financial management and procurement policy guidance.

* **Financial Management:** All financial flows will be regularly monitored and strictly governed by a set of contract and institutional arrangements under the ring-fenced facility implementation structure, and subject to the terms and conditions of the Agreement between the CCF and the KGB. Any expenditure in account pursuant to the general provisions of the FAA and AMA, or the Accredited Entity, KGB will regularly conduct an independent review of the use of funds and ensure that all aspects of the proceeds are being appropriately used in compliance with contractual and institutional arrangements.

* **The facility is subject to a credit limit of USD 10 million.** The CCFF Lending Facility is managed by the MCA of the CCFF Lending Facility, with loan disbursements requiring unanimous approval of the CCF and dual sign-off from the KGB and the MCA. All loans are disbursed in accordance with the terms and conditions of the CCFF Lending Facility and have a dedicated senior Operations Manager to ensure the process for authorizing payments is followed correctly and that all required documentation is submitted in compliance with the CCFF’s governing documents. In addition, EE will have a dedicated PMO who monitors at the TA activities under Component 1 and 3 and reports it to KGB.

In accordance with the IDP, the GCF may disclose the funding proposal without the consent of the entity if it determines that disclosure would not prejudice the confidentiality of the information contained in the funding proposal. # Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP228

## Proposal name: Cambodian Climate Financing Facility

### Accredited entity: Korea Development Bank (KDB)

### Country/limit: Cambodia

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

The Cambodian Climate Finance Facility ("CCF" or "Facility") is designed to mobilize finance for low-carbon and climate-resilient projects in Cambodia, a least developed country.

CCF is a two-part program: a concessional lending facility and technical assistance facility to be capitalized and operated by the KDB. The lending facility will provide direct concessional financing to qualifying projects in the form of long-term concessional loans and will also provide wholesale funding for other financial institutions (FIs) to make such loans. The technical assistance component will be used to establish the Facility and to support capacity building, project preparation, and implementation.

The Facility has a target capitalization of USD 100 million, while the Technical Assistance Facility will be funded by grants to a total of USD 15 million.


---
**Page 128**
---

1. The Technical Assistance Facility has similar tranches. GCF will contribute USD 0 million, split equally between tranche 1 and tranche 2. The remaining USD 8 million in co-financing will be contributed by KBR and ARBID (USD 3 million from ARBID and USD 1 million from KBR; both as equity). This amount is subject to the availability of corresponding commitments from co-financers for the Lending Facility.
2. The grant funds will be implemented by MSC as the executing entity and in a first instance with public sector entities. MSC will also undertake preparatory work on GCF and capacity-building of ARBID staff to support the project leading actors. Additionally, MSC will work with public sector entities to ensure that they have the capacity to capture LCFs and private sector entities on managing climate projects.
3. In the Lending Facility, KBR serving as the accredited entity, will direct loans to MEF, which in turn will disburse them to project implementers. The loan will be secured by a fenced account at ARBID. MEF, ARBID, and MSC will jointly function as executing entities for disbursing funds to project implementers. The loan will be structured as a 10-year loan with a 2-year grace period and a 5-year amortization period. The loan will be used to finance projects that qualify. The major share, eighty percent of the loan component, is earmarked for direct lending through MEF. This portion will be allocated to the project pipeline under-lying term loans and revolving credit facilities. The remaining portion will be allocated to whole-sale lending through LFBs. These lines are designed to bolster the development of Cambodia’s low emission pathways (LEPs) and support the implementation of projects that are aligned within GCFF and the projects it supports. As capacity grows, it is anticipated that ARBID will progressively take on more direct lending.
4. The funding program anticipates that the programme will yield a climate impact, reducing 11.1 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2 eq) and benefiting 1.3 million direct and indirect beneficiaries. This represents an increase in ambition from the initial GCFF climate change, along with more robust physical infrastructure resilient to the impacts of climate change.
5. GCFF is Cambodia’s first green financing initiative at a nationwide scale. It is also described in the funding proposal as a first step to enabling ARBID’s transition to a fully-fledged na- tional development bank. The project document for this proposal is 120 pages long and more, including eventual acceleration with GCF.
6. Limitations: This project document stretches over 120 pages which is more than double the proposed maximum length of a GCF funding proposal. This is not unique to the project at hand as longer proposals are commonplace. We would like to see the GCF Secretariat and ARBID consider relaxing this requirement in future GCFF funding proposals.
7. Impact potential: Medium-High
8. GCF intends to support projects in target sectors that mitigate or adapt to climate change. The near-term focus lines are renewable energy, energy efficiency, sustainable land use and management, and water resources. These sectors will be given a higher degree of allocation to each of these sectors, but will instead have a flexible approach to origination and portfolio development. This is because the GCFF project pipeline is still in its early stages with sector factors. This uncertainty, albeit normal for facilities such as GCFF, makes it difficult to evaluate the impact potential of GCFF with any degree of specificity.
9. The mitigation outcomes are expected to be driven by avoided emissions from the avoidance of over 11 million t CO2 eq and reaching 1.3 million beneficiaries. The mitigation outcomes are expected to be driven by avoided emissions from the avoidance of over 11 million t CO2 eq and reaching 1.3 million beneficiaries. These sectors have been identified as priority mitigation sectors in the updated na- tionally determined contribution (NDC) of Cambodia Moreover, these sectors are detrimentally
