## Impact of Limited Access to Finance

### Areas of Concern for the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP)

15. An area of initial concern for the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) involved ARDB's limited experience in non-agricultural sectors. As noted, CCF will operate across a variety of countries and sectors, including energy, water, and transport. The TAP is concerned with where the involvement of MICS as a co-executing entity of CCF will be instrumental. MICS's role will be to provide technical assistance in areas such as project design, implementation, and monitoring.

16. We commend CCF's plans to launch a request for proposals process to support development of a more robust project pipeline for the Facility.

### Issues with Cambodia

17. In contrast, we are concerned about the situation in Cambodia, a small landlocked country with limited access to capital markets and a limited ecosystem of local project developers. Many local developers struggle to secure financing from international financial institutions (IFIs) due to the high risk profile imposed by Cambodia. The funding proposal does a credible job of detailing the specific barriers to finance that CCF seeks to address. In summary, finance is not readily available for low-carbon and resilience projects in Cambodia. This is partly because of the country's weak institutional capacity and its general perception of high risk and challenging collateral requirements.

18. CCF is designed to mitigate some of these challenges by providing a dedicated credit line with concessional terms such as discounted interest rates and long-term loans.

### Credit Facility

19. CCF will have an Oversight Committee with a dedicated sub-committee (Green Credit Committee) that will be responsible for the oversight of actions under the CCF Lending Facility. These are aligned to its targeted results (impact, outcome, output). This is a critical function to ensure that CCF's lending activities are in line with the GCF's strategic objectives and contribute towards achieving low-carbon and resilience outcomes.

20. Funding institutions that require a dilution of their financial returns or a reduction in their risk appetite will not be able to participate in this Facility as it is designed for projects where the financial returns at end are nil. Standards could quickly degenerate if, as an expediency, impact or operational considerations were given precedence over financial considerations and resulted in potentially low coherence as regards impact gains. With this in mind, we recommend that the GCF Secretariat take an active role in ensuring that membership on the Green Credit Committee is well vetted to ensure a diverse range of expertise. This should also include members from other funders (second phase) on their committees to broaden the diversity of expertise.

21. We commended the tranches approach and the linking to co-finance. The co-financing nature of this facility is critical as the goal of CCF is not limited to funding approved projects but also includes capacity building, technical assistance, and knowledge sharing. ARDB has extensive experience in the relevant sectors. A barrier to climate finance in Cambodia is the lack of expertise and capacity within the country. This can be addressed by ARDB's capacity being wholisticated through other LFs that CFF can crowd-in other market participants. Moreover, Synergy Fund (SIF) will also provide a co-financing mechanism for projects under 10% of GCF.

22. We would encourage the CFF team to also see themselves as market makers. The concessionality available through CCF, alongside the credibility of ARDB, will provide the CFF management team with the opportunity to conduct market-making efforts such as organizing

# 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

## Scale: Medium - High

### Cambodia faces several barriers to climate finance due to the small size of its local financial sector, lack of long-term financing, and the lack of relevant expertise.

CCFF is intended to address these barriers by providing a dedicated investment vehicle that combines a market-based approach with an innovative public-private partnership model. The CCFF Lending Facility will have a dedicated governance and investment policy to derive the maximum impact from its investments in Cambodia's development goals.

### CCFF aims to provide climate finance directly into high priority sectors as identified in Cambodia's updated NDC and into LiFs to spur their investment into the same sectors. The facility is designed to be a catalyst for private sector investment, including through co-investments with other investors. In addition to the direct finance provided, CCFF aims to institutionalize climate governance at the national level by providing technical assistance that shifts its finances and builds local capacity to implement high climate impact projects in ARBIs.

### CCFF is not a one-off project but instead is designed to catalyze impact across the Cambodian financial sector. By building on existing expertise, CCFF aims to support the development of specialist projects in achieving bankability and growth. CCFF is expected to contribute to a paradigm shift in Cambodia's financial sector by helping to overcome market barriers and changing perceptions of the commercial viability of climate projects. If successful, CCFF will help reduce market barriers for climate finance in Cambodia through increased awareness, education, and enlarging the project developer ecosystem so that more climate-friendly projects can reach fruition in a timely manner. As more projects are developed (and financed by the private sector), CCFF will be able to scale down its own financing.

### Additionally, CCFF can make a significant contribution to a paradigm shift in Cambodia by building on existing expertise and experience of the Green Climate Fund in providing institution capable of designing, issuing and administering climate finance products. We also note that CCFF is intended to support ARBIs in GCF accreditation, which would provide a channel to clim finance for ARBIs and help build their technical capacity and financial management capacity of LiFs and the private sector to mobilize climate finance from public institutions.

### CCFF will also engage with relevant stakeholders including the Government of Cambodia on policy dialogues and sharing of lessons to contribute towards an improved enabling environment for climate finance in Cambodia.


---
**Page 131**
---

21. We note that CCF incorporates many green bank principles, and the funding proposal claims that the Facility will be the first application of the green bank model in Cambodia. Although, in our view, CCF is not strictly a green bank. It does model many of the attributes of green banks, including:

   23. The paradigm shift capacity of green banks hinges upon their adeptness in overcoming investment obstacles by strategically deploying meagre public resources to catalyze substantial private capital inflows. This approach is often referred to as the “green bank” model, whereby it not only champions environmental causes but also upholds financial viability as a foundational principle. The CCF, however, does not explicitly define itself as a green bank and genuinely deems green banks and possess limited potential for paradigmatic change. While the design of the CCF is commendable in terms of its innovative approach to financing commercial entities, they should nonetheless be adequate to ensure the financial robustness, creditworthiness and resilience of the projects it finances.

   24. The importance of a return requirement fosters greater discipline, potentially green banks could serve as an important collaborator for other co-funding sources.

   25. CCF will also contribute to scaling up and attracting supplementary capital from diverse collaborations as it evolves. The commendable tranche mechanism whereby funds from GCF are allocated based on deployment milestones and co-financing commitments is a key feature of the CCF funding proposal.

   26. This strategic approach not only facilitates the presentation of more credible funding proposals but also maximizes the potential for leveraging additional resources, thereby enabling greater tapping into valuable GCF resources.

   27. By demonstrating the feasibility of low-carbon investments across the target sectors, CCF will contribute to further scale up and address other green sectors suffering from similar barriers. Following the development of increased capacity and experience at ARDB, the institution will be better positioned to provide technical assistance to other financial institutions enabling them to offer the necessary catalytic support to the private sector, thereby facilitating the significant replication of low-carbon investments.

   28. CCF will also contribute to development pathways in Cambodia that are consistent with national climate change adaptation strategies. CCF directly responds to those needs by providing a flexible and innovative financing mechanism for climate change adaptation projects. The Facility will therefore contribute to funding the incremental costs of adaptation projects and demonstrating that adaptation actions in Cambodia can be financed by instruments other than grants.

   29. We note positively that most of the GCF funding requested is in the form of a loan facility which requires co-financing from private sector entities, including commercial banks. This pragmatic approach is in line with green bank best practices and an indicator that the project sponsor has considered the need for blended finance.

   30. We also observe positively that CCF, like other green bank efforts, will be well positioned to develop and diversify market knowledge in the local financial sector as well as to drive cross-sectoral collaboration. The Facility will contribute to the development of ARDB staff capacity as well as contributions to learning for LFs and private-sector actors through co-financing arrangements. This is a positive feature that should be supported in building up capacity to better understand climate impact, risks, and how to apply such insights into project preparation and implementation processes.

   31. Lastly CCF can also function as a trusted partner to cross-border capital providers. One of the impediments to attracting finance into developing markets from international private sources is the lack of trust in local financial institutions. This can be resolved by having a local co-finance partner that understands local political and economic realities.


---
**Page 132**
---

### Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: Medium-High

CCF’s objectives are aligned with Cambodia’s development goals. Under CFF, climate-friendly projects and businesses in Cambodia will have greater access to affordable finance. This is likely to lead to increased investment in renewable energy, including solar power, wind, agriculture, and energy transport. In this way, CFF can contribute to Cambodia's NDC targets while also supporting the country’s economic recovery and long-term growth. Other CFF direct benefits include avoided LULUCF emissions and improved access to finance for projects in the climate sector.

The CCF proposal credibly demonstrated its potential to contribute to the sustainable development of Cambodia beyond mitigation and adaptation impacts, including:

- Economic co-benefits: CCF is designed to deploy and mobilize climate finance, which in turn will support economic growth. The project will directly and indirectly create one million jobs (Sustainable Development Goal, SDG II). We expect that the projects supported by CFF will have a positive impact on employment and be engineered. Furthermore, many of the financed projects will have embedded climate resilience effects, which leads to economic co-benefits especially in the agricultural sector. For instance, drought-resistant crops can increase yields while also reducing water use; improved land degradation and deforestation will allow for increased productivity and crop yields for farmers; and agroforestry systems can provide additional income streams from non-food crops such as teak to decrease diversification in farming.
- Social and gender co-benefits: Adapting to climate technologies and best practices will support the achievement of Sustainable Development Goal 7 (SDG 7) and prevent waterborne diseases through clean water (SDGs 3 and 6), effectively improving the health and well-being of house-holds. One of CFF’s key gender co-benefits is to ensure that women are included in projects scrutinizing the MBDI website, revealing an exclusively male composition within both the Board and the Project Committee. This is a significant step forward for gender equality. Nevertheless, it is essential to underscore that CFF itself is conceived as a ring-fenced account, endowed with its own budget and governance structure (i.e., the CCF Oversight Committee and a CCF Working Group functioning as the project management unit) for the CFF Lending Facility.

The decision to mainstream gender equality, the executing partners affirm their commitment to mainstreaming gender consideration into this governance framework. The CFF Gen-der Action Plan is designed to ensure that women are included in all aspects of the pro-gramme targets designed to encourage women’s investment in decision-making roles. These targets include a minimum of 20 per cent women representation on the CFF Oversight Committee, a similar target for the Project Committee, and a target of at least 15 per cent women and 30 per cent women employment at the CFF programme level. Furthermore, the CFF Gender Ac-tion Plan will be reviewed by the CCF Oversight Committee (primarily) and the project management unit it is financed by.


---
**Page 133**
---

### 1.5 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: Medium-High**

#### Cambodia
- **Context**: A least developed country with low economic development making the financing of mitigation and adaptation interventions challenging.
- **Finance needs**: The climate finance needed to address major constraints to private investments will be essential for Cambodia's sustainable growth. CCFF will focus on sectors which suffer from underinvestment due to the market barriers, such as agriculture, forestry, and water resources.
- **Agriculture**: Climate change is expected to increase the frequency of extreme weather events, including floods, landslides, cyclones, extreme heat and wildfire, affecting in particular the vulnerable rural population. According to the funding proposal, climate change can put 61 per cent of Cambodia's agricultural land at risk (source: World Bank, 2015 [available on request]).
- **Forestry**: Much of the Cambodian population lives in rural areas and is highly dependent on climate-sensitive livelihoods such as agriculture, forestry, fishing, and livestock. Climate change will increase vulnerability and can hamper the country's sustainable development and NDC implementation.
- **Water resources**: The funding proposal estimates that Cambodia requires USD 7.8 billion of financing to meet its NDC targets in water resources, which is significantly higher than the country’s financial resources. With insufficient public funding and lack of private sector participation, the CCFF programme will provide a critical source of finance for climate change adaptation projects related to agriculture, energy, and water.
- **Long-term concession leasing**: The Facility has the potential to mitigate long-term impacts on climate project impacts that would not have been implemented without this programme.

#### Overall
- **Independent TAP rating**: The needs of the recipient are rated as medium-high.


### 1.6 Country ownership

**Scale: Medium-High**

The Royal Government of Cambodia has also provided a no-objection letter through its national designated authority (the Ministry of Environment, MoE). The MoE has been involved in the design of the CCFF programme from its inception having been supported by GCF through

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scale: Medium-High

The establishment of CCFF aligns with the GCF Private Sector Strategy. The proposal is also well-aligned with the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2014-2027, particularly as it regards to private sector engagement (e.g., through blended finance and co-financing). The CCFF will be the first national climate financing facility in Cambodia; private sector financial institutions will be actively involved in carrying out worthless lending or co-financing projects, as well as helping to mobilize private sector finance at scale by extending support to LPLs, private sector entities, and rural households. From our perspective, CCFF offers an efficient approach to amplifying private sector climate action.

In addition to its private-sector focus, the CCFF assumes a pivotal role in enhancing the climate resilience of Cambodia’s public sector. The CCFF will be designed to mobilize private sector finance at scale by extending support to LPLs, private sector entities, and rural households.


---
**Page 135**
---

climate investment in Cambodia, leveraging green bank principles, and collaborating with the well-established and strategically positioned ADB. Notably, partnerships with LFs serve as an effective means to reach end-beneficiaries, affirming the viability of climate projects.

In we also note that LFs in Cambodia exhibit risk-averse trading practices, necessitating substantial financial support from other sources. This is further compounded by limited availability for climate projects in Cambodia, coupled with capacity gaps and a limited track record in cli-mate finance. In this context, the CCFF aims to address these challenges by providing technical assistance, heightening vulnerability of infrastructure assets to climate change risks in Cambo-dia, supporting the development of project-level risk mitigation strategies, and assessing impacts, as well as incorporate adaptive climate adaptation into infrastructure project assessments, pose substantial challenges for LFs. The CCFF will provide a dedicated amount of concessional finance needed to enhance the cli-mate resilience of renewable energy projects in Cambodia.

CCFF, through its blended finance approach, aims to address these impediments, offering direct support to the private sector and public institutions in Cambodia. As a Facility nature as that is blended finance instrument is intended to alleviate barriers such as tenure, high per-ceived risk, and limited access to financing for renewable energy projects. The CCFF will also structure transactions where commercial banks can participate and will report on its progress toward the goal of mobilizing co-investment.

This provides us with comfort that the programme is reasonably well designed and adapted for the Cambodian context. It is important to note that the CCFF does not provide financing that are not yet bankable without credit enhancement, financial or development support.

CCFF also intends to use GCF concessionality to offer highly competitive financing rates directly to project developers in Cambodia. The CCFF will work with the Ministry of Finance on mapping the interest rate environment in Cambodia and justifying the discounted rates that CCFF intends to offer. This will be done by comparing the cost of capital for non-concessional funds and risk mitigation solutions, coupled with specific technical assistance initiatives. These efforts aim to bolster their ability to accurately discern, evaluate and formulate cli-mate finance projects, which are then eligible for co-investment or direct investment at the project level.

Overall, the rationale for the independent TAP was that CCFF represented a major upscal-ing of ADB's assets. Based on a desktop review we identified that ARBIS has a relatively modest loan portfolio (USD 120 million roughly) and not much more to total assets. If the CCFF were to meet its target of USD 364 million in concessional lending, it would represent an increase in loan volume and loan capacity compared to those previously managed by ARBIS, posing an execution and ca-pacity risk for ARBIS. The CCFF is expected to mobilize a total amount of USD 159 million over a period of five years (i.e. USD20 million per year) to meet projected CCF lending volumes. Given the phased approach to scaling up, the CCFF would be able to increase its loan volume by more than asset in the last five years (2018, USD 159 million increased to USD 364 million in 2022) according to the annual budget. The CCFF is expected to mobilize a total amount of USD 159 million as an asset, assuming the growth rate of per 20 percent is maintained, will be less than 10 per cent in 2024. Therefore, we judged that the risk that CCF could represent a disproportionately large portion of the overall ARBIS portfolio and that ARBIS would not have sufficient capacity to manage it, es-timately a ring-fenced facility with its own dedicated operational and governance structure.

In conclusion, the CCFF is expected to play an important role in catalyzing private sector investments in support of climate interventions in Cambodia. We also view favorably the amount of co-fi-nance and the variety of co-finance partners that are engaged in discussions for phase two fund-ing to demonstrate a commitment by the public sector to leverage private sector investment, and to catalyse an additional dollar at the Facility level. Moreover, each dollar infused into the CCFF into its funded projects is anticipated to attract supplementary private, commercial and institutional


---
**Page 136**
---

capital at the project level, constituting a pivotal point for leverage. The Funding Proposal envisages the Facility mobilizing USD $47 million in investments for Cambodia's NDC priority sectors. CCFP is poised to contribute 50 per cent of the necessary investments for the green projects it supports. Given that the average loan yield for commercial banks in Cambodia hovering between and 3.5 per cent per annum, it becomes imperative for CCFP to provide concessional loans at below 3.5 per cent per annum in effectively leveraging their participation in green financing.

Furthermore, direct beneficiaries of green projects require annual rates averaging between 1 per cent and 2 per cent, which is not feasible with the current interest rates offered by commercial investors. Considering the anticipated concessionality of CCFP along with green loan yields and operating costs, the resultant return on assets for CCFP is estimated to be around 1 per cent, showcasing its viability as a financial institution. It is important noting that CCF concessional benefits are passed on to both wholesale and direct borrowers.

Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The CCFP programme aligns closely with the established principles of green banks, which have demonstrated effectiveness in delivering crucial financing for climate mitigation and adaptation. We post that CCFP has the potential to unlock avenues for both public and private capital to contribute towards the achievement of Cambodia's NDC targets. This is achievable through the direct provision of financing and assistance to qualifying climate interventions, as well as by leveraging ARBDS.

Significantly, the commitment of CCF funding will be paralleled by corresponding financial support from ARBDS, symbolizing a tangible national dedication to this initiative. We commend the Government's commitment to ensuring that the full potential of CCF funding is contingent upon CCF achieving substantial deployment and on-financing milestones.

In our assessment, the CCF concessional loans and technical assistance play a pivotal role in diminishing the costs and risks - both real and perceived - associated with investments in climate-responsive projects. By providing these resources to eligible projects, CCFP aspires not only to deploy its own capital but also to galvanize private investment, thereby exponentially increasing the scale of climate action.

The ring-fenced configuration of CCF stands as a safeguard for the programme's funds, preventing any commingling with other operations or assets/liabilities of ARBDS. This structural delineation ensures that CCFP remains focused on its core mandate - providing financing in climate-responsive projects meeting the eligibility criteria outlined in the programme.

The Stakeholder Engagement Plan, encouraging the involvement of Indigenous Peoples, women and youth, as well as the inclusion of gender considerations in project design with regard-related targets on women's representation and employment, are well noted.

### Impact potential

The Accredited Entity (AE) would like to acknowledge the ITAP’s recognition of Programme target sectors aligned with Cambodia’s National Determined Contribution (NDC). The Programme has been designed in response to the identified needs in Cambodia to attract climate finance and accelerate NDC implementation. Through a close partnership with Executive Director, KDB, and the Asian Development Bank (ADB), the AE will support the development of Cambodia's low-emission, climate-resilient development pathway through upscaling and private sector financing.

### Paradigm shift potential

The AE would like to express gratitude for recognising that the Programme is intended to address local market barriers to climate finance, with an advanced blended finance structure in response to the needs of the public and private sectors within Cambodia’s financial sector. The Programme provides long-term, blended finance to local financial institutions and private sector entities to bridge knowledge- and technology gaps hindering the uptake of targeted climate projects in Cambodia.

### Sustainable development potential

This is well noted. The Programme will contribute to Cambodia's NDC implementation and a number of economic, social, gender and environmental co-benefits.

### Needs of the recipient

The AE agrees with ITAP that Cambodia faces several barriers to deploying climate finance, including limited access to funding and lack of private sector participation. The programme has been designed to tackle those funding gaps through long-term concessional lending and enhance capacity across the finance, development, and public sectors, eventually contributing to Cambodia's NDC target.

### Country ownership

The Programme will enable ARDF’s transition to a fully-fledged national climate development bank with direct access to international climate finance for Cambodia. # I. Introduction: CCFF’s Commitment to Gender Equity

The Cambodian Climate Financing Facility (CCFF) is a dedicated national climate finance facility to contribute to Cambodia's low-emission and climate resilient pathway. It is specifically designed to leverage much needed public and private sector funding to accelerate Cambodia's NDC implementation through:

- Catalyzing funding at scale for key climate mitigation and adaptation sectors: sustainable land use and management, renewable energy, energy efficiency, water resources management, agriculture, and water.
- The CCFF expects to mobilize US$300 from private and public sector investors. The investment will be complemented by a US$1 million TA Facility.
- Addressing gender gaps in climate finance through the development of long-term and concessional financing to Local Financial Institutions and Projects Development to de-risk high, climate impact potential projects.
- Providing grant funding to facilitate capacity building and knowledge sharing.

The CCFF acknowledges that women and men equity contribute to combating climate change, and gender responsive approach is a part of the CCFF's paradigm shift approach to contributing to Cambodia's low-emission and climate resilient development pathway.

To this end, the CCFF shall promote and support gender equity by institutionalizing gender responsive approach at a level of Institution and projects we finance. CCFF operations will be guided by the Gender Policy and the Gender Action Plan.


This gender gap assessment builds upon the desktop review of secondary data, as well as stakeholder consultations conducted during conceptualization of the CCFF facility with public and private stakeholders in Cambodia, including the Ministry of Women Affairs of Cambodia and other members of the Royal Government of Cambodia, the Cambodian Sustainable Finance Initiative (CSFI), of the Association of Banks in Cambodia, the National Bank of Cambodia, and the World Bank. The purpose of this gender assessment is to understand the country level and sector specific gender gaps, so as to decide on entry points for CCFF investments. This gender gap assessment will be complemented by the project-specific gender assessments (see Annex Bb). This gender assessment will be complemented by the Project Specific Gender Assessments to be prepared during project development phase. The CCFF will also consider and address gender risks and opportunities including through stakeholder consultations. Based on findings of project specific gender assessments, borrowers will be required to establish gender action plan which will lead to an improved understanding of gender gaps in climate finance investments, as well as promoting and enhancing gender equity and women’s economic empowerment (see Annex Bb for CCFF level gender-disaggregated indicators and targets).

The Green Climate Fund's Gender Policy and Gender Assessment Template (Annex B) was consulted for preparation of this assessment report. so was the number of international best practices including that of the ILO and OECD.

Gender assessment process undertaken to prepare this document and determine entry points for the CCFF’s gender responsive climate finance approach is summarized under figure 1 below.

The respective project specific Gender Action Plan will be developed on a need basis.

# Gender Assessment Process

## Key Demographic Trends in Cambodia

### 2.1 Key Demographic Trends

The key demographic trends provide information on Cambodia's gender-disaggregated data across Cambodia's population households, marital status, education, and health as of the year 2019.

#### Population

As per the 2019 census, women comprised 51.3% and men comprised 48.7% of Cambodia’s total population of 15,296,489. Between 2008 to 2019, the population in Cambodia increased by the 2,156,529 persons, which represents an increase of 16.1% and an annual average growth rate of 1.4%. The vast majority of the population (86%) lives in rural areas; furthermore, there are slightly more women than men in Cambodia.

Households and societal norms:

The average household size in Cambodia was 4.3 persons in the year 2019, a decline from 4.7 in 2008. This decrease in household size is due to shift from joint and extended family structures towards smaller, family-oriented households. The female heads of household outnumbered male heads by more than two to one.

The majority of the female heads of household were aged between 30-59 years, majority of them were economically active. The studies report, that an increase in family size and head of household’s education level increases the likelihood of female-headed households.

| Male Led Households | 74% |
|---|---|
| Female Led Households | 26% |

Figure 2: Household Characteristics by Gender

Marital status:

Most of the population in Cambodia aged 15 years and above (66.2%) were married. The never married population is 27.5%, while the proportion of widowed/divorced/separated population is 6.3%. However, there were more women than men who were widowed or divorced (40% vs. 31%). This is due to a higher rate of male mortality, as well as indication that man tend to remarry more than one woman in most cases*. Females in Cambodia tend to marry earlier than males. The average marriage age was 27.0 and 24.1 years for females and males respectively.

The proportion of married women aged between 35-49 years old is higher (68%) compared with the average age at marriage in 2019 had increased by 1.4 years and 0.7 years for males and females, respectively. The average age at marriage was 24.1 years for males and 22.5 years for females.

The proportion of married women aged between 35-49 years old is higher (68%) compared with the average age at marriage in 2019 had increased by 1.4 years and 0.7 years for males and females, respectively. The average age at marriage was 24.1 years for males and 22.5 years for females.

Marriage status is the most important determinant of women's wage employment. Women who are married are 38% less likely to be in paid employment*.


Education:

Cambodia is on track for achieving gender equality in education and literacy, as per the Cambodia’s voluntary national review of implementation of the 2030 agenda for sustainable development.

Nonetheless, the Global Gender Gap index indicates that the number of challenges remain in terms of education attainment, as Cambodia ranks 128 out of 158*. The key challenges to be addressed are: (1) equitable and inclusive access for the children at all levels of education and training (2) transition from primary to lower-secondary and high-drop out rates at the lower secondary level. (3) quality issue from pre-

primary to tertiary educators, (4) life-long learning, which is a key to life-long employment and allow adoption of skills and knowledge to the structural change of the economy".

The relevant indicators:

* **Literacy Rate:** 88.5%

The proportion of literate persons among the population aged seven and older increased from 78.4% in 2003 to 88.5% in 2019. However, there are still noticeable gender gaps in terms of literacy rate (91.6% of males and 85.3% of females). The gap is larger for the age group aged 15-17 years old (94.5% of males versus relative to men. It should be however highlighted, that the literacy rate varies by the age group. The male and female literacy rates are very close to each other for younger age groups (close to 90%), but are but are wider in older age groups.

The proportion attending school/educational institution

More than half of the population in age groups 6-11 years and 12-14 years were attending school in 2019, while only 25.7% for age groups over 18-34 and 8.7% of age group above 25 were attending educational institution. The 2019 census reveals that only 29.1% of those who ever attended school or educational institutions had received the secondary diploma, while 6.5% of those who ever attended school or educational institutions had received the tertiary diploma and 2.8% had completed education beyond secondary level.

The gender gap assessment reveals, that while enrollment rate for girls was marginally higher than enrollment rate for men at ages 6-11 and 12-14, the girl's enrollment rate declines relative with boys with age. For example, in the age group of 15-17 years old, the school attendance ratio is 0.9 for males but for the age group over 25, the school attendance is 0.8 for male and 0.6 for women. This school/educational institution drop-out rate is a reflection of this aspect. This reflects traditional attitudes that discourage girls from continuing to study after a certain age.

Covid is expected to further affect gender gap in education. Surveys by various organization found, that distance learning disproportionately affects female learners than male learners. Girls and young women are especially engaged in housework and unpaid care work for younger siblings and older members of the family while home, which affects their time to undertake online learning classes.

The latest mortality rate and under-five mortality rates in Cambodia is higher in males than females, which is due to the biological differences. The life expectancy at birth is higher for females than male. The mortality rates estimated for rural areas are higher than those of urban areas. The differences are due to the level of parental education, access to health facilities, and poor hygienic conditions. Chronic diseases and mental disorders are among one of the leading causes of sickness and death in rural children in Cambodia.

The maternal mortality ratio is 141 per 100,000 live births. Malnutrition is considered the underlying cause of 45% of child deaths and 20% of maternal deaths in Cambodia. Furthermore, 32% of the children in Cambodia are stunted due to malnutrition. The lack of access to clean water, sanitation, and hygiene (WASH), poverty resulting in sub-optimal feeding and a mother's lack of education are major causes of child undernutrition, which is one of the most significant obstacles to human development.

### 2.2 Women Economic Empowerment

The women economic empowerment was benchmarked against publicly available data for women in businesses (interpreted as women headed by women) and the women in labor force. The gender-sensitive wage gap was also mapped.

The analyses revealed that women economic empowerment is limited and there is a room for growth both in terms of women in business and women in labor force. Most women are working in informal economy, which is characterized by low productivity, low income, and low business capacity on microenterprises, with profit generation capacity of such enterprises being lower than that of large and medium-sized enterprises.

We also found that women capacity to participate in labor force both in terms of education and skills is limited, with Cambodia's national strategic development plan pointing to a need to promote gender equity in the labor and vocational training1.

The COVID-19 crisis has accelerated automation and digitalization, which increased labor market disruption. The 2020 Gender Gap Index for Cambodia points to significant challenges for gender parity in the future of jobs due to increasing occupational gender-segregation. Furthermore, less than 3% of the Cambodia's women entrepreneurs have access to formal credit.

The review of secondary data also revealed that social norms and gender roles persist barriers to women's economic empowerment. Women are more likely to be engaged in unpaid domestic work. Although women are nominally guaranteed equal rights with men1, unlike their male counterparts, women are seen as having a lower status in society2. Traditional and gender norms assigned to girls and young

### 2 | 28


---
**Page 148**
---

women (early marriage, household chores, taking care of younger siblings, etc.) result in limited educational opportunities. Consequently, women often enter the labor market with fewer educational qualifications and skills than men. See section 2.3 on barriers to female participation.

These same social norms and obstacles prevent women from accessing customers and new markets.


The women employment is concentrated in sectors traditionally associated with women's work, such as agriculture, education, health care, and domestic services. Women are underrepresented in the labor force in energy, industry, and transport sectors; so is the promotion which tends to be restricted by general norms and institutional settings (see section 2.3), with fewer women holding decision-making roles in Cambodia[1].

### Women in Business:

There were 753,670 enterprises in Cambodia as of 2022, and 99% of enterprises in Cambodia are MSEs (enterprises with up to 100 employees). Female representatives account for 62% of all enterprises in Cambodia. However, women are underrepresented in large enterprises, while men are underrepresented in energy, water, and transport sectors, while overrepresented in agriculture and manufacturing sectors. The large enterprises and SMEs have the lowest women representation, of 21% and 26%. While the micro enterprises, have highest woman representation of 60% (Source: UNCDF 2021; IFC).

(particularly to women microentrepreneurs) and non-financial services (e.g., skills training, networking, advocacy, organizing trade fairs/exhibitions) to enhance capacity/market access. Yet, the various studies performed by ADB, ACED, AEU, UN Women Cambodia, and others have consistently emphasized that women-led businesses in Cambodia face barriers to growth. The key barriers include lack of financial resources (e.g., loans, credit), limited market access, poor infrastructure, and policies also associated with socio-cultural norms as discussed in previous sections) and less training/educational opportunities for women. This is further evidenced by the fact that women microentrepreneurs are more likely to be engaged in informal enterprises than men. Women-led businesses is also evidenced by Cambodia's recent census data, which emphasizes that larger the scale the enterprise is, the higher proportion of male representative. On the other hand, findings indicate, that women who own small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) tend to have more access to finance. More investments in women-led business represent an opportunity to enhance gender equality and women economic empowerment, as well as improve social inclusion.

Business Regulations Only 12% of enterprises in Cambodia are formalized, as the process to formalize business is complex and time consuming in Cambodia. While this is the problem for both men and women, the women's tendency to be less educated and social norms opt women from registering business in Cambodia. According to the 2019 Socio-Economic Survey of Cambodia, only 35% of all registered businesses in Cambodia are microenterprises. Furthermore, sole proprietorship is also a most common form of business ownership among women in Cambodia (80%). The SME segment therefore is very small (2.1%), with large enterprises representing less than 0.6% in Cambodia. This data suggests barriers for growth and presence of "missing middle".

Women in Labor Force: According to Cambodia's Socio-Economic Survey 2019, the total working-age population is around 8,036,799 people. The share of women is slightly lower than men, with women's share at 49% and men's share at 51%.

| SHARE OF WOMEN IN LABOR FORCE | 48.8% |

Figure 4: Cambodia's Working Age Women and Men in Labor Force

The labor force of Cambodia is largely informal**, as paid employment in Cambodia constitutes only 31.5% (23.6% male and 39.7% female). The majority of the employed are informal (67.5%), largely constituting of own account workers (42.7%) and unpaid family workers (15.1%). The proportion of females in this informal sector is higher than males, with women being more likely to be self-employed or dependent workers. In the case of females, most reported working as unpaid family workers (35.4% female versus 15.1% in male). In rural areas, the proportion of unpaid female workers is even higher (62.2 percent).

As noted in earlier sections, marital status is an important determinant of women's wage employment. Women who are married are 39% less likely to be in paid employment. Larger family size and higher education levels in heads of households increase the likelihood of women becoming wage workers.

In 2019, the majority of employed persons in Cambodia's economy were working as agricultural, forestry and fishing workers (53.4 percent[16]), followed by craft and related workers (16.6%) and services and sales workers (12.6 percent). This indicates that Cambodia’s labor market is still predominantly composed of unskilled workers. The female participation in agriculture, forestry and fishing was 70% compared to 58% for males have always been higher in rural areas than in the urban areas for male and females—largely driven by higher poverty levels and lack of access to education in rural areas. The female participation in skilled agriculture, forestry and fishing was higher (15.1 vs. 10.1%).

The economically inactive population includes people who are not working and not seeking work during the reference period. The proportion of the population who are not economically active is higher among females (41.2 percent) than among males (34.6 percent).

Gender Wage Gap:

The gender wage gap between women and men is **11.8%**, the wage widens across CCFF key priority investment sectors such as agriculture and renewable energy (See table for specific gender pay gaps). In 2019, the year earned on average USD 294 per month, women earned USD 256 per month**. The observed factors contributing to this wage gap is gender disparities in education and employment opportunities. The proportion of women employed as managers is one of the most important determinant of women's wage employment. Women who are married are **38% less likely to be in paid employment**.

### Gender Gap in Social Protection:

The women coverage under existing national social assistance program and social security scheme is limited**, women are often left without any support. Low social protection is especially felt with regard to old age pension, disability allowance, and child benefit schemes. However, there has been progress in NSDP (2019-2023) and there are plans by the Royal Government of Cambodia (RGC) to widen the coverage.

### Gender Gap in Managerial Positions:

In 2019, **3 out of 10 managerial positions were occupied by women**, while men occupied the **7 out of 10 posts**. This is a reflection of limited opportunities and empowerment issues for women in leadership positions as further explained under section 2.3 below.

#### 2.3 Women in Leadership and Decision-making Roles

The women in decision making roles were benchmarked against publicly available data on gender statistics in political, civil service and judiciary sector. While women participation has been growing in institutional setting and the royal government of Cambodia has been encouraging women participation, the women participation is still very low compared to men. Women are underrepresented at all levels of decision-making opportunities. Women's participation in decision making is limited for a number of reasons, including lack of education and skills, they are not called to high positions and decision making. Women’s role in caregiving and household work is yet another barrier to their full participation in decision making, as is women’s very low level of education**.

### Women in Political Leadership Roles:

The Parliament of Cambodia is the bicameral legislature of the Government of Cambodia, consisting of the Senate and the National Assembly. The figure below indicates that the number of women senators has only increased from 10% to 25%, while the number of women MPs has increased from 8% in the first mandate (2013-2016) to 14%. Women were accounted for 15 percent, and the fourth mandate (2019-2024) is 16 percent. It shows that women's situation in the Senate remains largely unchanged, thereby emphasizing on need of women empowerment in political roles.

Women make up about 16% of the government senior position from Under Secretary of State to Deputy Prime Minister (as of February 2020).

Women in Judiciary:

Women's participation in the judiciary system is relatively low within seven years of service (2013-2020). Women constitute only 15 percent of judges, 14 percent of prosecutors, 22 percent of lawyers, and 12 percent of the notary public (as of February 2020).

Women in Civil Service:

Even though the numbers of male civil servants are still higher, more women civil servants have been recruited, increasing from 32 percent to 41 percent from 2007 to 2018. This is largely due to Royal


---
**Page 153**

Government of Cambodia’s introduced quote, with a guideline to include 20 percent to 50 percent of women in the annual recruitment of the public institutions.

### 2.4 Gender Based Violence

Violence against women (VAW) is still a pervasive issue in Cambodia. Among women aged between 15-64 in Cambodia, 30% have experienced violence in physical, sexual, psychological, and economic forms.

The National Survey on Women’s Health and Life experience in Cambodia further revealed that approximately one-third of Cambodian women had experienced physical or sexual violence by their intimate partner at least once in their lifetime. Most of the women reported experiencing violence also claim that the incident occurred more than once. Furthermore, 75% of women experienced psychological violence from their intimate partner during their lifetime.

The survey also found that 20% of women’s first sexual intercourse experience was without their consent.

While non-partner VAW is not as common as intimate partner, it is still of significant concern in Cambodia. Non-partner physical violence was reported by 14% of women; friends, parents and siblings were most commonly identified to be the perpetrators. The 6% of Cambodian women has experienced economic violence.

According to USAID study28, a culture of impunity protects the perpetrators rather than the victims of violence, corruption, and abuse. Key challenges are: (i) the lack of mechanisms and guidelines for effective implementation of laws, policies, and programs; and behaviors, which underlie gender based violence; and (ii) the stigmatization of survivors of violence.

The recent studies also warn that, the COVID-19 pandemic may further increase women’s exposure to violence, as a result of measures such as lockdowns and disruptions to vital support services28.


### 2.5 Socio-Cultural Norms in Cambodia

The Cambodian household is traditionally headed by a man as evidenced by the 2019 household data under section 2.1 “Households”. The family is at the core of society and plays a key role in meeting the economic, social, cultural, and spiritual needs of its members through production, consumption, and child caring. Various studies indicate, that compared to many other women in Asia, the women in Cambodia play a major role in household decision making.

While Cambodian men have traditionally been seen as the breadwinners and the protectors of women, they have also assumed roles traditionally reserved for men, perhaps as a legacy of Khmer Rouge period29.

Culture, traditions, and beliefs have contributed significantly to the construction of gender identities and social norms in Cambodia. Men are traditionally portrayed as strong, rational and powerful; whereas women are often described as gentle, emotional, weak, and humble30. The moral codes (Chhila Sreah and Chha Phale) also underpin such stereotypes. In accordance with these behavioral codes, Cambodian women are

### III. Institutional Assessment

Cambodia ranks 103 out of 156 in the Global Gender Gap Index 2021. As per the index, the gender gaps remain large across all dimensions (48% for health and political empowerment gap at 129). The progress in economic empowerment has improved in line to the country's economic growth; however, the gaps still remain in particular to women’s paid employment (rank 46). The table below provides a summary of performance against Cambodia’s SDG5 targets. It is important to note that the index does not capture all dimensions of gender equality and it points to gaps with regard to the women economic empowerment and women in decision making. Our findings in data 8 on line are the key gender gaps pointed out by the global and local rankings.

#### Table 3: Summary Performance Against Cambodia SDG5 on Gender Equality

| Indicator | Score |
| --- | --- |
| Empower woman in wage employment | Unfinished business |
| Empower women in decision making | Unfinished business |
| Combat violence against women | No significant improvement |

To come into the root causes of gender gap, this institutional assessment section focuses on analyses of legal framework and policies vis a vis gender equality in Cambodia.

#### 3.1 Legal Framework

The Cambodian Constitution, Article 31, recognizes that women and men have equal rights to participate in societal, economic, political, and cultural lives. Articles 35 and Article 45 of this constitution also emphasize equal work with equal payment among men and women, prohibiting all forms of discrimination against women.

#### Text Box 1: Extract from Constitution

**Article 21 (rights and freedoms):** Every Khmer citizen shall be equal before the law, enjoying the same rights, freedoms and fulfilling the same obligations regardless of race, color, sex, language, religious belief, political tendency, birth origin, social status, wealth, or other status.

**Article 35 (political equality):** Khmer citizens of either sex shall be given the right to participate actively in the political process, including the right to vote and stand for election.

**Article 45 (discrimination against women):** All forms of discrimination against woman shall be abolished, and the exploitation of women in employment shall be prohibited in marriages and matters of the family. This constitutional commitment is also reinforced by Cambodia's ratification of the Convention on Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1992; and its signing of the "Platform for Action" agreed at the 1995 Fourth World Conference on Women in Beijing.


---
**Page 155**
---

The employment relationship in Cambodia is governed by a number of legal instruments such as the Constitution, the Civil Code[1], the Labor Law[2], the Law on Minimum Wages[3], the Law on Social Security[4] Schemes, the Law on Trade Unions[5] and international treaties incorporated as part of Cambodian law through parliamentary enactments.

The labor law does not allow discrimination in wages based on sex, origin, age, and caste[6].

[1] Cambodian Civil Code, promulgated on 8 December 2007 and fully implemented in late 2011
[2] Labor Law No. 34/2009 as amended on 25 July 2011 and on 26 April 2013
[3] Law on Minimum Wages, dated 6 July 2018
[4] Law on Social Security Schemes, dated 7 December 2019, replacing the old law dated 2002
[5] Law on Trade Unions, promulgated on 17 May 2018 (amended on 3 January 2020) and its implementing regulations.
[6] Source: https://www.ilo.org/cambodia/lang/en/index.htm

3.2 Policy Framework

The Royal Government of Cambodia (RGC) adopted Cambodian Sustainable Development Goals Framework (2016-2037) and localized Sustainable Development ("Gender Equality") into action through (i) Rectangular Strategy (NSDP 2019-2025), (ii) National Strategic Development Plan (NSDP 2019-2025), (iii) Nearay Rattanak Strategic Plan 2019-2023 and (iv) National Action Plan to Prevent Violence Against Women (NAVPAW). These key strategic policy frameworks are followed by the monitoring and evaluation of progress towards gender equality.

The gender mainstreaming is noted in all these strategic frameworks, however benchmarking to international best practices reveal that some of those frameworks may benefit from more granularity on how to overcome inherent social and sector specific gaps.


| Table 4: Summary of Key Policy Frameworks to Institutionalize Gender Equality in Cambodia |
| --- | --- |
| **Policy Framework** | **Key Components** |
| Rectangular Strategy (NSP) | (i) Human resource development (ii) Private sector and market development, and (iii) Sustainable and inclusive development. |
| National Strategic Development Plan (2019-2025*) | The NSDP is the strategic planning framework, which is the backbone of the sustainable development efforts of Cambodia and focuses on overcoming binding constraints, including gender equality. The NSDP sets out a series of strategies for achieving progressive improvement in socioeconomic conditions, mobilizing resources and monitoring progress. |
| Nearay Rattanak Strategic Plan 2019-2023* | The NSRP addresses gender mainstreaming in five strategic areas (i) Women's Economic Empowerment; (ii) Education and Skills Development for Girls and Boys; (iii) Legal Protection for Women and Girls; (iv) Women in Public Leadership and Politics; and (v) Gender Inclusive Climate Change. |

*Source: https://mop.gov.kh/en/DownloadFile?fileID=18-396; 4541-6643-8257402319


---
**Page 157**
---

National Action Plan to Prevent Violence Against Women 2018-2023 (NAPVAW)

NAPVAW was developed and approved by the IOM to reduce violence against women and girls in Cambodia. It is a multi-sectoral, multi-stakeholder approach that provides a response, increased access to quality services, and multi-sectoral coordination.

Four key areas of focus are: (i) Prevention; (ii) Legal Protection; (iii) Multi-Sectorial Services; and (iv) Coordination and Implementation of Laws and Policies. It reviews Monitoring, and Evaluation. It provides direction for implementation at the national level as well as at the provincial and district levels through the work of national institutions at all levels.

In terms of governance, it is noteworthy that the working group on NAPVAW is chaired by the Prime Minister.

Climate Change and Gender Action Plan related initiatives

The National Strategic Plan on Gender and Climate Change 2014-2023

Action Plan on Gender and Climate Change 2019-2023

Despite the constitutional right for gender equality as well as progress in terms of gender focused policies and strategic plans, mainstreaming gender equity in Cambodia is still facing constraints. In particular, the key policy frameworks reveal following gender challenges in Cambodia:

i. Most women in Cambodia are working in the informal economy and the industrial employment within this sector is characterized by low pay, lack of support for capacity and skill development, strengthened social protection and safety at work.

ii. The skill and capability of women's participation in economic activities remains limited, and the opportunities to access education and training are still very limited. Women have less access to services related to time availability and access to information, production resources, finances, and technology.

iii. Women traditionally are required to balance both paid and family care work, and consideration is needed of the fact that higher social costs are incurred on their activities and time. Flexible support and childcare services are needed for working women in order to achieve a better life balance, which can be the encouraging factor for more effective and productive work.

iv. Gender stereotypes reinforce the idea that women should prioritize family care over paid work, which hinders the development of women's roles in modern society. Women have been encouraged to embark on careers in the informal economy or in small-scale agriculture rather than in the formal sector, which has not enabled women to select and decide for jobs following market demands. In addition, this has led to a limited number of women who study or work in Science, Technology, Engineering, Art, and Mathematics (STEM) fields.

v. Negative gender stereotypes and attitudes in the family, the workplace, and the public space continue to be barriers to women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels. These are barriers to enjoyment of their rights and their potential development.

vi. The health sector is still facing many challenges, especially in remote areas with a focus on expanding information and understanding of the importance of health care, hygiene, and nutrition.

vii. Women and girls face physical, sexual, emotional, and economic violence that may occur at home, at work and in the community, and all forms of violence may be perpetrated against women and girls by family members or strangers.

viii. There is an identified need to strengthen and expand the partnership mechanisms with the private sector for gender equality.

ix. Gender stereotypes reinforce the idea that household duties and unpaid care work falls under the responsibility of women, and this is still a major barrier to women being able to exercise their rights and participating in public works and holding leadership positions.


---
**Page 158**
---

## 3.3 Governance

The national institution responsible for gender mainstreaming in Cambodia is the Ministry of Women’s Affairs (MOWA). It was established in 1996 to lead, coordinate and facilitate efforts for gender equality and women's empowerment in Cambodia. The ministry works with public institutions, civil society and private sector actors on gender equality and women’s empowerment issues as part of its role under the Royal Decree No. A Neary Rattana Strategic Plan. MOWA has created gender mainstreaming action groups (GMAGs) in each line ministry, who are mandated to support women's economic empowerment by preparing and implementing gender-sensitive policies.

It is noteworthy that the MOWA is a member of the National Council for Sustainable Development (NCSD) and the National Committee for Disaster Management (NCDM). It is thereby involved in advocating and addressing issues related to gender equality, disaster risk reduction and climate change as well as the policies on green growth development, climate change, disaster risk reduction and impact management.

It has subcommittees “Committee for Gender and Climate Change” (CGCCC) responsible for developing national guidelines on gender mainstreaming in climate change programs as well as capacity development of civil servants at all levels. So far, the CGCC of MOWA has also developed guidelines for gender mainstreaming in adaptation in five sectors (Agriculture, Rural Development, Health, Water Resources, Public Works and Transport).

## IV. Gender Assessment per Sector and Entry Points for CCF

We conducted sector specific gender assessment in Cambodia; however, we were constrained by availability of gender disaggregated data on energy, transport and water sectors. This gender assessment is high-level and used as an entry point for planning CCFR gender-mainstreamed programmatic approach. It is for information purposes only and does not represent a comprehensive gender analysis or mainstreamed investment decision making and project design. See the CCFR Gender Action Plan for details.

### 4.1 Financial Inclusion in Cambodia's Financial Sector

Financial inclusion is increasingly being recognized as a key driver of economic growth and reducing inequality in Cambodia. This section provides an overview of financial inclusion in Cambodia and proposes the key entry points for developing CCFR gender-mainstreamed programmatic approach for wholesale and bottom-up (micro-finance) interventions.

# Key Findings

## Financial Inclusion in Cambodia

### Bank Credit
- **10% of women** interested to take loan from bank (2019)
- **70% of men** interest (2019)

### Account Ownership
- 21.5% of women and 21.8% of men in Cambodia have an account (as at 2017)

### Asset Ownership
- 51.4% of men and 61.5% of women owned a savings account or deposit with a bank (2014)

## Geographic Distribution

- **74%** of the population is in urban areas.
- **69%** in rural areas.

### National Bank of Cambodia
- 33% of the population remains excluded from formal financial services provided by microfinance institutions and payment service institutions (source: National Bank of Cambodia).

## Need for Financial Deepening

- There is a need for financial deepening in Cambodia through banking sector, to enhance access to credit and product offerings.
- Findings demonstrate that banked population in Cambodia is still low compared with the global average (52% vs. 67%), and only 10% of women have access to credit from a bank.

## Financial Inclusion Strategy

- The Royal Government of Cambodia's (RGC) Financial Sector Development Strategy, which was launched in 2018, outlines key objectives for the financial sector.
- Furthermore, RGC’s Financial Inclusion Strategy outlines a need to promote innovative credit products and services that are tailored to meet the needs of women entrepreneurs.

## Women Entrepreneurs

- **Women** represent majority of business owners in Cambodia (as at 2018).
- However, only 35% have access to formal sources of finance to grow enterprises.
- Only 25% of women are banked, and only 3% of women SMEs have access to formal credit mainly served by microfinance institutions.

## Affordability of Loans

- Affordability of loans is an issue in Cambodia, for both women and male-owned businesses.


---
**Page 161**
---

- Banks in Cambodia have a track record in lending to women, but there is limited institutional capacity and human resources to mainstream gender into climate finance: The executing entity has a track record of working with local financial institutions in Cambodia in partnership with USAID/CCF. However, the majority of banks in Cambodia are not yet fully committed to mainstreaming gender into their operations. Member banks to develop Gender Policy, E&S risk management policies and tools, and develop green lending strategies that promote women-owned businesses and/or business that contribute to gender equity as well as appetite to enhance climate finance to women-led business and/or business that promote gender equality and women economic empowerment. The capacity of financial institutions in Cambodia with the capacity and knowledge gaps are currently limited, banks ability to branch into gender mainstreamed climate finance. Preliminary engagements with banks also highlight the need for institutional and human resource capacity building to enhance gender equity.
- For example, eight banks that were already provided with E&S risk management tools (including gender) have not yet fully integrated these tools in their operations. The CCFP programme provides technical assistance to implement Gender Policy. The CCFP programme is specifically designed to meet this need by providing financial and non-financial support in form of gender expertise to develop and implement project specific Gender Action Plan (see Annex Bb) to its borrowers in collaboration with the CCFP.
- **Entry Points for CCFP in Financial Sector:**
  - Wholesale Loans to Local Financial Institutions: Gender-mainstreamed investments to local financial institutions will contribute to climate finance by providing loans to women-owned businesses that contribute to gender equity and women economic empowerment. Thereby, enhancing credit and product offerings and contributing to financial sector deepening in Cambodia through climate finance.
  - Institutional and human resource capacity building to enhance gender equity: Given capacity and knowledge gaps in Cambodia's financial sector to promote uptake of gender mainstreamed investment tools, the CCFP programme will provide technical assistance to banks. The available TA funding resources will provide non-investment support to enhance gender equity and women economic empowerment considerations at institutional and project level. The capacity building will include conducting a gender assessment, develop gender action plan, build institutional capacity to mainstream gender into banks operations, and provide technical assistance to implement the gender action plans. The CCFP is targeting women customers. Active collaboration with Cambodian Sustainable Finance Initiative (CSFI) and National Bank of Cambodia is planned. See Annex D for detailed entry points for the financial sector.

# Sustainable Land-use and Agriculture Sector

CCFF shall invest in sustainable land-use and agriculture activities, with forest conservation features.

To this end, we have assessed the existing gender gaps in forestry and agriculture sectors under text box 2:

## Text Box 2: Gender Assessment of Cambodia's Agriculture and Forestry Sectors via a vis planned CCFF Sustainable Land-use and Agriculture Investments

The forestry and agriculture sector in Cambodia contributes to less than 20% of GDP or the year 2019, however the proportion of employed population engaged in the sector is around 40%. The majority low productivity and low income earning women are concentrated in the forestry and agriculture sectors.

The agriculture is the sector where most Cambodian women and men work, however the majority is vulnerable employment (own account workers and unpaid work), as only 1.1% of men and 0.7% of women aged between 15 or older are engaged in formal employment in agriculture. The wage gap in the sector is 14.3%.

It is noteworthy that, the agriculture sector itself is characterized by low productivity in Cambodia, which affects women's income generating capacity disproportionately then men.

Women in agriculture face variety of obstacles and constraints compared to their male counterparts including access to economic resources, knowledge, and capacities to invest in appropriate agriculture technologies and services. The majority of female farmers are smallholders with less than 2 ha landholdings in Cambodia have difficulty to access extension services and finance. The gender gap persists in access to land ownership and inheritance rights. Only 10% of agricultural households were headed by women household in 2018. However, only 11.7% of agricultural land were owned by woman-headed households and remaining 88.3% owned by male-headed households.

It is noteworthy that approximately 34% of agricultural households reported experiencing some form of severe weather events during the last 12 months, including weather events like typhoon, flood, landslide or drought. As a matter of fact, weather related events are one number most reported shock (82%), with drought and food security being the second most common shocks experienced by the farmers.

The most common responses to these shocks included selling crops and livestock, finding work outside the agricultural holding, or borrowing money. This indicates that agriculture household holding's income (both men and women) is highly vulnerable to weather related events and other shocks, especially when the constraints to access to economic and financial resources.

In terms of forestry, the commercial forestry is predominantly male dominated in Cambodia, while the women are discouraged from pursuing leadership roles in the sector. It also noteworthy that there is a lack of women representation at all levels of decision-making and policy-making processes. As per USAID study, "women are often not invited to grassroots, sub-national, and national level meetings, and men usually hold the leadership positions and choose who (to join or not) to invite".

### References

2. *Robust* data on women's participation in agriculture was engaged in agriculture, forestry and fishing at 33.1%, followed by both manufacturing and wholesale and retail trade at 41.7% which are then by construction at 48.2%. It is noteworthy that out of 50.9% of the employed population aged between 15 or older were engaged in agriculture, forestry and fishing (63.5% for men to women). Remaining 30.5% of employed persons aged 15 or older are engaged in informal work. It is also noteworthy, the majority low productivity and low income earning women are concentrated in the forestry and agriculture sectors.
