Entry Points for CCFF in Sustainable Land-use and Agriculture

The gender and social inclusion entry points in the forestry and agriculture sector given CCFF’s sustainable land-use and agriculture investment plans are as follows:

- Investments encourage sustainable land-use and agriculture, by addressing productivity constraints in agriculture and the sector in which most Cambodian women work.
- Promote women’s economic empowerment through access to finance and other resources, including through job creation and financing of women-led businesses in the agriculture sector.
- Promote gender equality and social inclusion in the forestry and agriculture sectors. This includes promoting women’s participation in sustainable land-use and agriculture practices, as well as training women employees on forest conservation and sustainable land use practices (to be offered by the entrepreneurs financed by the CCFF).
- Enhance women’s economic empowerment through access to finance and other resources, e.g., through stakeholder engagement as well as encouraging women in leadership positions.

See CCFF Action Plan for additional details on sector specific targets and actionable items.


4.3 Clean Energy Sector (Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency):

CCFF shall invest in renewable energy (solar, wind), and energy efficiency measures (energy efficiency in manufacturing sectors). The gender assessment of Cambodia’s energy and manufacturing sectors will be highlighted; there is a relative scarcity of gender related studies focused on clean energy in Cambodia. To this end, this sub-section also discusses relevant data with broader context of energy sector and manufacturing sector.


Text Box 3: Gender Assessment of Cambodia's Energy and Manufacturing Sectors, vis à vis Planned CCFF Clean Energy Investments

The gender assessment of the clean energy value chain was benchmarked against three key indicators: (1) access to energy services; (2) economic empowerment in energy and manufacturing sector; (3) stakeholder engagement. The findings reveal that sector specific and broader structural issues discussed under Section II result in gender inequalities.

Access to energy: The Royal Government of Cambodia made remarkable progress in terms of energy access, which currently stands at 84%. Nonetheless, there is gender gaps in terms of access to energy. As per world bank 2016 data*, 69% of male-headed households have grid access, compared with 65% of female-headed households. This means that women are more likely than men to be living without electricity. Women and girls spend a large part of their day collecting fuelwood for domestic purposes and production, which translates in inequality as well as into lost opportunities for education and remunerated labour**.


*Source: https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/bitstream/handle/10986/2801/124490.pdf?sequence=5&isAllowed=y

** 57% of population in Cambodia still uses firewood for cooking. In the rural areas, about 88 percent of the households used firewood as fuel for cooking. Source: Cambodia Social Economic Survey 2019/20


---
**Page 164**
---

Economic empowerment: The clean energy value chain in Cambodia is gender biased*, with gender differences not captured in economic statistics. This is often the case for other developing countries too.

To put this in perspective, only 0.2% of the Cambodian working age population is employed in the electricity, gas, steam and air conditioning supply* and manufacturing sectors**, as well as women's engagement in clean energy business.

We found that only 0.2% of the Cambodia’s working age population is employed in the electricity, gas, steam, etc., and manufacturing sectors. This is significantly lower than the global average of 13%, compared with 24.3% who were females. There is significant wage gap of 40% in the industry. It is also noteworthy that the women make up less than 33% of technical staff and below 31% of managers in Cambodia.

While exact statistics for clean energy sector is not available, we assume that similar low employment and significant gender gap may persist in renewable energy, largely due to the broader structural issues in Cambodia (see Section II) that prevent women’s ability to enter and advance in their careers. These structural issues are: (1) limited formal employment and wage discrimination; (2) capacity constraints given the lack of access to education beyond secondary and university education, especially STEM; and (3) access to capacity enhancement trainings***.

Social norms and cultural perceptions of women are yet another significant barrier. For example, there is observed evidence in Cambodia that women are successfully leading renewable energy micro enterprises, but they are not able to scale up their businesses due to lack of capital. Women’s opportunities for expansion is limited due to household obligations and limited access to finance.

In terms of the manufacturing industry****, we zoomed into garment industry as an energy efficiency measures will be focused on this industry. We found that job in garment industry is viewed as an opportunity for economic empowerment for women. The garment industry has a large female workforce with 80% being female garment worker population however holds a line leader or office staff role. Male workers are more likely than females to hold leadership positions. There is also evidence of an historical preference for female workers for lower skilled jobs****. Out of 3 women experience sexual harassment in the workplace, 68.2% were also made to feel uncomfortable or unsafe in the workplace.

Stakeholder engagement: Drawing on project experiences in grid-connected renewable energy in Cambodia, we found that gender equality and social inclusion are often not considered in sectoral perceptions limiting women’s ability to participate at every stage of energy decision-making. In Cambodia and as demonstrated by the Decision II, high-level policy and service delivery decisions are predominantly made by men. Furthermore, women dominate under section 2.5 further hampering their participation in public consultations for RE projects and thereby, limiting the ability of women to influence decisions on the quality and potential outcomes of the project. It is also noteworthy that land acquisition process for large scale renewable energy projects can be lengthy.

*Cambodia’s updated NDC identifies a need to enhance monitoring and evaluation systems of sectoral metrics to go beyond employment statistics. The gender equality and social inclusion indicators are not captured in existing data, gender indicators and budgeting, outcome-based reporting and the disaggregation and scaling of the gender and climate change attention within the sector.

****There is no official document describing the statistics of employment or business ownership in energy-related sectors.

**Furthermore, there is a lack of information on women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels. The statistics show that transfers were made both men and women in the sector in Cambodia, contributing to 17.3% of employment of working age population in Cambodia: 22.2% women and 17.5% men. Majority of jobs in manufacturing industry (19%) is informal employment.

***Source https://www.care.org.au/wp-content/uploads/2017/04/SnC3_SnF_Women-Cambodia_Garment_Industry_March_2017.pdf


---
**Page 165**
---

projects are mostly domain of the men, as women households have less title to land then men in Cambodia. Yet, women engage with land and access energy in ways different than men and the women's participation is constrained.

### Entry Points for CCFF in Clean Energy

Based on the gender assessment in the sector, CCFF key entry points for the gender equity and women's economic empowerment in clean energy sector is as follows:

- Investments encourage gender mainstreamed infrastructure design, with gender assessment being mandatory for all renewable energy projects considered for financing for CCFF funds. The gender analysis forms part of the analysis for scoping and feasibility studies for solar/wind installations, mini-grids, biogas plants, etc., to ensure that women's needs are met in line with the disabled, the elderly, indigenous groups and all local communities impacted by the proposed infrastructure. Gender equality quick impact measures (GEQIMs) and gender employment are included in the gender action plan.
- Large scale renewable energy projects provide opportunities for direct empowerment of women and men. Quotas for female employment, together with gender sensitive workplaces, separate male and female toilets, and PPE designed for women are envisioned entry points as per the CCFF Gender Action Plan (GAP).
- Encourage workforce safety, and harassment free work environment through project specific gender action plan.
- Ensure that women's needs are met in line with the disabled, elderly, indigenous groups and all local communities impacted by proposed infrastructure.
- Collaborate with private sector through knowledge sharing events to strengthen capacity across clean energy value chain.

See CCFF Action Plan for additional details on sector specific targets and actionable items.

### 4.4 Low-Carbon Transportation Sector

CCFF shall invest in low carbon transportation sector. Since the gender disaggregated data in low-carbon transportation sector is not available, we will focus discussion on observed information in transportation sector in Cambodia.

#### Text Box 4: Gender Assessment of Cambodia's Transportation Sector, vis a planed CCFF Low-Carbon Transportation Investments

Cambodia's transport sector is male dominated, with motorcycles being the most popular choice in Cambodia (accounting for more than 85% of vehicle registration in Cambodia). While the gender disaggregated data in terms of access to transport is not available, we assume that in line to other countries in Asia, women are less likely to own a vehicle. However, it should be noted that women and men in Cambodia also have higher access to income and control over family finances, while women in Cambodia are viewed as more responsible for household spending than their male counterparts. The mobility needs of women and men are also different[1]. To this end, the men in Cambodia are likely to benefit more by an increased access to transport both in terms of mobility as well as affordability.


---
**Page 166**
---

Traffic accidents in Cambodia are high, especially from motorbikes which accounts for 75% of fatalities by the transport sector**. Much of case burden of the injured due to the gender norms fall to women and girls, given their greater reliance on motorbikes as a mode of transportation.

There is a lack of human resources, qualified technicians and skills to take advantage of the advancement of technology and innovation in transport sector in Cambodia as per the NSDP**, which effects women and men employment and career advancement in transport sector disproportionately**.


Entry Points for CCFF in Low-Carbon Transportation

CCFF's key entry points for gender equity and women's economic empowerment for low-carbon transportation sector is:

- Gender mainstreamed access to low-carbon transport, given affordable financing offered by the CCFF. The number of additional female and male passengers using low carbon transport as a result of CCFF support is as follows: [39% total passengers; 20% female passengers];
- Increased employment for women. The increased access to mobility for women can also lead to entrepreneurship in the sector;
- The purchases of motor vehicles for commercial use by enterprises will require safety trainings to employees, thereby promoting safe access to transportation.

See CCFF Action Plan for additional details on sector specific targets and actionable items.


4.5 Climate Resilient Water Sector

CCFF shall invest in climate resilient water, focused on rural areas. The discussion is thereby focused on gender gaps and opportunities in water sector.


Text Box 5: Gender Assessment of Cambodia's Water Sector, via a vis planned CCFF Climate Resilient Water Investments

The gender assessment in climate resilient water sector was benchmarked against three areas: (1) access to water; (2) economic empowerment in water sector; and (3) decision making in water sector.


Access to Water

In terms of resilient and adaptive water supply and sanitation remain scarce in Cambodia's rural areas. In 2018, while 74% of rural households had access to improved water supply, only 10% of those had piped water supply and only 5.7% of rural households had access to hand washing facilities connected with running water from a piped system. The water shortage during the dry season (November-April), with increased droughts under future climate scenarios to further result in water inequality. In rural areas, women are responsible for sourcing of water, which is a time-consuming task and often results in poor health as well as income-generating activities. Women are also primary users of domestic water given their household chores and involved in agriculture sector. As a result, they are more vulnerable to water-related diseases such as typhoid fever, leptospirosis, melioidosis, viral hepatitis, and schistosomiasis given limited access to resilient and adaptive water supply.

Economic empowerment in water sector: We have analyzed Cambodia's most recent labor census and found that women make up 10% of the Cambodian water utility workforce. This is lower than other sectors such as agriculture and employment trends. We found, that only 0.2% of the Cambodia’s working age population are employed in the sector. Women represent a mere 3% of the total number of workers in this sector. Despite women's high employment in the sector, however, there is a 11% wage gap. The industry is dominated by the low-skilled workers of both men and women. In Cambodia, only 20% of the population have a university level education held by women. The Water and Sanitation Utility Services 2018-19 gender brief identified that women make approximately 10% of the Cambodian water and sanitation utility workforce.

Privately owned and managed rural water schemes are being promoted by the Royal Government of Cambodia (RGC) as a means to improve access to safe drinking water in rural areas and funding. There are however gender gaps impacting entrepreneurial opportunities for women in rural water supply schemes. The women entrepreneurs are limited by a “double burden” of household care and income generating activity, which is often unpaid work. Lack of education and training, lack of financial resources, and childcare are impacting profitability and discouraging women economic empowerment in WASH sector[8]. Technical training, business literacy training and education was identified as yet another barrier impacting economic empowerment.

Decision making in water sector: The weather shocks impact water supply and infrastructure in Cambodia, with women affecting disproportionately as they are the primary users of water for domestic use. As women are more likely to be involved in decision-making processes related to water resources, their participation in public consultations for water projects and thereby, off-setting the ability of women to influence decisions on the quality of the water supply project design (inc climate resilience) and potential outcomes of the project.

Entry Points for CCFF in Climate Resilient Water

CCFF’s key entry points for gender equity and women's economic empowerment for climate resilient water sector is:

- Gender mainstreamed design of water supply projects: specific quotas for female participation in stakeholder engagement are included in the gender action plan.
- Increased access to climate resilient and improved safe water supply in rural areas, that brings health benefits (including for women) who use water in ways different to men).

### V. CCF Gender Responsive Approach to Climate Finance

The Cambodian Climate Financing Facility (CCFF) is a dedicated national climate finance facility to contribute to Cambodia’s NDCs.

CCFF is specifically designed to contribute to inclusive climate action, supporting Cambodia to implement its climate ambition, gender equality and development goals as identified in their NDC, national development strategies and sector policies.

To this end, the CCFF shall promote and support gender equity by institutionalizing a gender responsive approach to lending for all projects we finance. CCF operations will be guided by the Gender Policy and the Gender Action Plan.


We identified key entry points for a gender mainstreamed approach for each sector financed by the CCFF in section IV of this document, however, we also acknowledge there are significant gender gaps existing in Cambodia’s climate change policy framework. The CCFF, as a dedicated national climate financing facility, shall set an example in terms of gender-mainstreamed governance operational procedures and investment processes.


Furthermore, the CCFF shall contribute to the knowledge transfer through collaboration with partner financial institutions to share gender assessment and monitoring tools, as well as lessons learned on gender responsive climate finance thereby contributing to the institutionalization of gender mainstreamed government policies. The CCFF will also support capacity building for women in the public sector to foster mainstreaming in private sector, as well participation in knowledge sharing events together with public and private sector counterparts to promote gender equality and knowledge transfer. In addition, CCFF shall contribute to gender disaggregated climate finance data collection in Cambodia, in line with Cambodia’s Master Plan for Gender and Climate Change (2018-2030).


For additional details on CCF's Gender Response approach to climate finance see CCF Gender Action Plan.

# Overview

This Gender Action Plan is divided into three sections.

- Section 1 provides information on CCFF Programme’s gender co-benefit outcome and explains how the CCFF Programme outcomes will be achieved through actions outlined in CCFF GAP. It also discusses the CCFF Programme's contribution to Cambodia’s Master Plan for Gender and Climate Change (MPGCC) (2018–2030).

- Section 2 presents the CCFF Gender Action Plan, which contributes to delivery of the CCFF Programme gender co-benefits and gender responsive climate finance through five outputs and respective indicators.

- Section 3 provides information on key entry points for CCFF’s sector specific interventions.


## Section 1: CCFF Programme Gender Co-benefit

The Cambodia Climate Financing Facility (CCFF) is a first of its kind, country-owned, climate finance facility to catalyse public and private sector funding for high climate impact projects that contribute to NDC implementation and to Cambodia's Master Plan for Gender and Climate Change (MPGCC) (2018-2030).

The CCFF Programme’s co-benefit outcome is increased gender mainstreaming into investments to enhance gender equity and promote women economic empowerment. This outcome will be achieved through the following five outputs and respective indicators of gender mainstreaming opportunities across each output of the CCFF Programme (see FP, output 1-5) as follows:

### 1.1 Institutional Preparedness of the CCFF

The CCFF is set up as a ring-fenced account with dedicated resources for climate change financing. Through MRV, thereby to integrate gender equality and economic empowerment opportunities in institutional preparedness of the CCFF, gender will be mainstreamed into the following areas: (a) project preparation and verification system; and (b) project implementation.

#### a. Project Preparation and Governance on the level of the CCFF

The achievement of Gender Mainstreamed Governance at the level of the CCFF will be achieved through:

- Adopting CCFF Gender Policy to communicate CCFF aspirations to internal and external stakeholders towards gender equality.
- Enhancing CCFF decision making process by setting specific targets, and
- Enhancing internal gender expertise at level of governance, by assigning respective roles and responsibilities for gender mainstreaming in CCFF operations.

#### b. Project Implementation

The achievement of Gender Mainstreamed Operational Process on the level of the CCFF will be achieved through:

- Adopting and training staff on an anti-sexual harassment policy.
- Implementing a non-discrimination and equal opportunities and support hiring retention and promotion of female staff, through setting specific measurable targets.


---
**Page 172**
---

### c. Gender Mainstreamed Monitoring, Reporting and Verification system: gender-disaggregated data collection, analysis and impact reporting will be promoted as per CCFF GAP

#### 1.2 Institutional Capacity Building of ARDB to Recruit a National Climate Bank in Cambodia (NCCB)

The NCCB will be responsible for the implementation of the CCFF’s Gender Mainstreaming Strategy, which is defined by the following:

- **Gender Impact Assessment:** The NCCB will conduct gender impact assessments as part of its project appraisal process.
- **Gender Budgeting:** The NCCB will ensure that 30% of its Loan Proceeds are allocated to projects with a high gender impact. These projects must meet specific criteria:
    - At least 50% of the borrowers must be women.
    - Have at least 30% women in senior management; and/or provide products or services designed to disproportionately benefit women.
- **High-Impact Projects:** The NCCB will set gender targets aligned with the 2x min industry targets for sectors targeted by the CCFF, together with a progressive human resources policy.
- For 30% of the portfolio to meet the criteria above (applicable for financial institutions only).

In addition, to mainstream gender co-benefit outcome into investment projects, each borrower funded by the CCFF will be required to submit a Gender Action Plan (GAP) as part of its Investment Project Implementation Plan, that will set actionable targets to promote gender equity and women economic empowerment at the level of project/program.

#### 1.3 Technical Assistance Facility to Address non-financial barriers hindering uptake of climate projects in Cambodia: CCFF shall dedicate 50% of grant funding to enhance gender mainstreaming into climate investment projects and contribute to institutional transformation. This will be achieved by:

- (a) Active collaboration with Cambodian Sustainable Finance Initiative (CSFI) and National Bank of Cambodia to organize knowledge sharing events and facilitate much needed dialogue on gender responsive climate financing between the public and private sector actors.
    - Such knowledge sharing events are expected to contribute to a more enabling environment for CCFF’s grant funding ready available to fund Cambodia’s climate finance policy framework and CCFF grant funding is readily available to fund


---
**Page 173**
---

CCFF contributes to Cambodia’s Master Plan for Gender and Climate Change (2018-2030) as follows:

* **Improves delivery of gender responsive climate finance:**
    * Directs the implementation of MPOCC's strategic objectives in the private sector.
    * Creates awareness of climate change and need of gender mainstreaming in climate planning/investment/mobilization process.
    * Combines lessons from disaggregated climate finance data collection in Cambodia and lessons learned.
    * Demonstrates gender related differential impacts of Climate Change (CC) and the benefits from integrating gender perspectives into CC policies, strategies, and plans.
* **Builds gender mainstreaming capacity in key priority NDC sectors, which are also identified in MPOCC:**
    * Enhances women's empowerment of women through job creation, capacity building and women-led business financing.
* **Organizes knowledge sharing events to enhance much needed dialogue on gender responsive climate financing between the government, civil society and private sector.**

# Output Activities Indicators and Targets Timeline Responsibilities Costs*
## Sustainable Finance Initiative
### Goal: Gender equality in climate finance
- Share lessons learned from CCFF Program
- Invest in gender mainstreaming of CC projects
- Disseminate regional knowledge on impacts of gender mainstreaming in climate finance
- Enhance access to key stakeholders
- Engage CSF Committees, Borrowers, and other members of CSFs
- Assess impact during program duration, based on evaluation

Section 3: CCFF Gender Responsive Climate Finance Entry Points per Sector

Below sub-sections identify key entry points for gender responsive climate finance interventions per sector according to country level Gender Assessment performed:

It should however be highlighted, that those are the entry points and as part of CCFF’s investment process, the CCFF team will perform the gender assessment of activities to be financed by the CCFF loan and grant components. This will include a review of the project design, including the gender analysis, gender mainstreaming and grant allocation process, see actionable item 3.1 and 3.2 above. Thereby, project specific gender vulnerabilities and risks, as well as opportunities for project specific gender impacts and outcomes will be already identified at investment stage and in fact, these findings will inform overall decision-making for the investments.

Entry Points in Sustainable Land Use and Agriculture Sector

• CCFF Investments encourage sustainable land-use and agriculture, by addressing productivity constraints in agriculture, the sector in which most of Cambodian women and men work but represent different roles. To ensure that women’s participation is not only recognized but also supported, all will be taken to ensure that workers employed by the borrower are provided the social and labor protections that they are entitled to receive by the national law. Furthermore, working conditions will be in line with ILO conventions and other international standards as well as national environmental law requirements.

• Promote women’s mainstreamed access to economic and financial resources and opportunities, including through creating formal employment and financing of women-led businesses in agriculture sector. This entry point will be translated into project specific gender action plans (GAPs) based on project specific gender assessment findings. Target: 40% of new jobs generated by the project are taken by women, to so align with Cambodia’s NSDP 2019-2023.

• Promote women’s mainstreamed access to land and resources for sustainable land use and agriculture practices, as well as training to women employees on forest conservation and sustainable land management. This entry point will be translated into project specific gender action plans that will define percentage of women and men participating in training and 25% of women and men that could apply lessons learned from the training to their own livelihoods. Target: 40% of beneficiaries are female, however ensured, that at least [35]% of the beneficiaries of training and capacity building are female in line to NSDP 2019-2023.

• Enhance stakeholder engagement practice, by ensuring that consultations are conducted in gender responsive manner with women’s groups and also represent women and vulnerable stakeholder engagement. If women participation is limited, encourage borrower to perform outreach events to increase women participation. Target: [50]% of engaged stakeholders are women and vulnerable groups11.


---
**Page 183**
---

# Entry Points in Clean Energy (Renewable Energy, Energy Efficiency) Sector

* Investments encourage gender-mainstreamed project design, so as to maximize women's equal access and benefits. Gender assessments are mandatory for all renewable energy projects considering their potential impact on women and girls' empowerment.
* The gender assessment forms part of the analysis for scoping and feasibility studies for mainstreaming into national and stakeholder consultations, which shall include women and women’s groups, the disabled, the elderly, indigenous peoples, and other vulnerable groups. A minimum 30% target quota for this vulnerable group participation in stakeholder engagement will be encouraged at 20%.

## Labor Management Procedures

* Compliance to IFC PS Z, encourage non-discrimination and equal opportunities and secure hiring, retention and promotion of women.
* Incorporation of Occupational, Health and Safety measures.
* Development of SEAH Policy Statement aligned to CCFP’s ESMS and other relevant national requirements with the borrower.
* Code of Conduct for contractors and sanctions against the use of GBV/SEAH is required for all contractors and subcontractors and their workers.
* Awareness raising sessions on GBV/SEAH information and awareness sessions on GBV/SEAH through training modules, workshops or staff induction sessions can also be organized for temporary workers employed in the project. The training can be verbal or written, depending on local context and literacy level.
* This type of awareness raising/training can be “training logs” to be signed by staff covered under activities.

## Grievance Mechanism

* Establish, maintain, and operate a grievance mechanism for Project workers & Community consistent with CCFP ESMS and relevant safeguard standards. This will include the establishment of clear procedures regarding GBV/SEAH and an accountability framework and mechanisms.
* Other protocols as it may be necessary depending on applicable SEAH risks
* Access to GBV service providers where GBV services are available, and how they will be referred and the services which will be available.


---
**Page 184**
---

### Entry Points in Low-Carbon Transportation Sector

- **Gender mainstreamed access to low-carbon transport**, given affordable financing offered by the CCFF. The number of additional female and male passengers using low-carbon transport will be measured, to ensure that targets set in the CCFF log frame are attained.
- Women's participation in decision-making on transport infrastructure indicators:
  - **Reduced average travel time for women and men** to essential services (e.g., health care facilities, schools, markets), and access to efficient markets, jobs, and other income-earning opportunities
  - Increased number of women in transport-related employment
- The purchases of motor vehicles for commercial use by enterprises will require gender mainstreamed safety trainings to employees, thereby promoting safe access to transportation.

### Entry Points in Climate Resilient Water Sector

- **Gender mainstreamed design** of water supply projects through gender inclusive stakeholder consultations, ensuring at minimum [50%] of female participation in stakeholder engagement.
  - Increased number of women and men accessing improved drinking water sources, including health benefits (including for women, who use water in ways different to men). Target: [50%] of all project beneficiaries reporting access to safe drinking water in rural areas are women by the end of CCFF.
- Increased employment and entrepreneurial opportunities for women in the water supply schemes, given that 75% of funding will be allocated from CCFF. Gender mainstreamed indicators will be determined in Borrower specific GAP.

### Entry Points for Financial Sector Interventions

Local Financial Institutions who seek funding from the CCFF will be encouraged to develop gender responsive approach to climate finance aligned to CCFF Policy and Outputs 1-5. With each Local Financial Institution Borrower, CCFF will perform gender assessment and agree on a Borrower Specific Gender Action Plan (GAP). This will help analyze the paradigm shift towards gender responsive climate finance. Below are possible entry points for GAP.

| Table 1: Possible Entry Points for Financial Institutions Gender Action Plan [2] |
| --- |
| **Output 1: Enhance Gender equality in Financial Institution's governance and leadership** |
| - Leadership committed to improve gender equality through financial inclusion |
| - Enhance internal gender expertise at level of governance, by establishing a gender focal point |
| - Conduct awareness seminars for FI Leadership Team (Board Management Team) |

[2] Source: ClimateAction (CAA) LLC., adapted for CCFF Programme. IP rights of CAA apply.


---
**Page 185**
---

### Output 2: Gender Mainstreamed human resource policies to enhance gender equality in workforce

- Adopt and train on an anti-sexual harassment policy with respective CCFF SEAT
- Promote non-discrimination and equal opportunities and support women-owned enterprises
- Collection and analysis of sex-disaggregated data
- Regularly review the progress towards goals to increase the number or percentage of female members on leadership teams and in management positions (e.g., board, executive team)
- Provide flexible work practices (e.g., flexitime, telecommuting, job sharing, part-time with benefits, compressed workweeks, etc.)
- Offer childcare services, parental leave, paid time off for return to work after parental leave, subsidized childcare, paid time and subsidies for dependent care, and other incentives to increase women's appointments with dependents
- Offer career progression pathways including through capacity building, education sponsorship or mentorship too, given persisting educational and technical skills challenges that limit the prevent progression of women on leadership roles in CCFF-supported enterprises
- Such actionable items will apply to wholesale loans supported by the CCFF, which specifically target women and women-owned enterprises

### Output 3: Gender mainstreamed lending policies to increase women's financial inclusion

- Develop easy-to-access solutions to enhance gender-mainstreamed lending (e.g., microfinance, credit scoring, gender assessment tool, webinars) for banks/SEATs
- Conduct regular reviews of lending practices to avoid bias in lending practices

### Financial products or services adapted to meet women's needs

- Create market campaigns specifically targeting women (e.g. for cash and savings)
- Adapt application procedures to meet women’s needs
- Provide financial education and training programs for customers

### Output 4: Gender Mainstreamed MRR

- Establish sex-disaggregated data and monitoring mechanism on gender mainstreaming in lending process
- Public awareness raising campaigns of gender mainstreaming in lending process

### Output 5: Knowledge Sharing

- Participate in knowledge sharing events on women entrepreneurship or gender mainstreaming in lending process.

In addition to above, the quarterly Reporting to the CCFF team on implementation of the Borrower specific GAP shall be included in the Loan Agreement.
